# Graph integrals, Feynman periods, and single-valued multiple zeta values

**arXiv ID**: 2607.25595v1
**Authors**: Jean-Luc Portner
**Published**: 2026-07-28
**Categories**: math.QA, math-ph, math.NT
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.25595v1

## Abstract

The Borel classes generating the stable cohomology of the general linear group can be represented by invariant differential forms. It is known that pulling these forms back along a tropical Torelli map yields canonical convergent integrals associated to graphs, which are closely connected to the cohomology of $\mathrm{GL}_n$ and of graph complexes. A natural question is what numbers these graph integrals are. We answer this for primitive canonical integrals by showing that they coincide with a family of complex position-space integrals arising in deformation quantisation. As a consequence, canonical integrals of graphs evaluate to single-valued multiple zeta values. We further deduce that every single-valued multiple zeta value occurs as a rational linear combination of Feynman periods of graphs with massless propagators. Finally, in the commutative graph complex, our result implies that the two associated cocycles agree.

## Graph integrals, Feynman periods, and single-valued multiple zeta valuesJean-Luc PortnerMathematical Institute, Oxford, OX2 6GG, United Kingdomjean-luc.portner@maths.ox.ac.uk

## Abstract.

The Borel classes generating the stable cohomology of the general linear group can be represented by invariant differential forms.
It is known that pulling these forms back along a tropical Torelli map yields canonical convergent integrals associated to graphs,
which are closely connected to the cohomology ofGLn\mathrm{GL}_{n}and of graph complexes.
A natural question is what numbers these graph integrals are.
We answer this for primitive canonical integrals by showing that they coincide with a family of complex position-space integrals
arising in deformation quantisation. As a consequence, canonical integrals of graphs evaluate to single-valued multiple zeta values.
We further deduce that every single-valued multiple zeta value occurs as a rational linear combination of Feynman periods of graphs
with massless propagators.
Finally, in the commutative graph complex, our result implies that the two associated cocycles agree.

## 1.Introduction

Letn≥3n\geq 3, and letG=(VG,EG)G=(V_{G},E_{G})be an edge-ordered graph with no self-loops,V:=|VG|=n+1V:=\left\lvert V_{G}\right\rvert=n+1vertices andE:=|EG|=2​nE:=\left\lvert E_{G}\right\rvert=2nedges i.e.E=2​V−2E=2V-2.
This work shows the equality of two families of integrals associated toGG: canonical integrals, obtained by
integrating over positive edge parameters, and RW-integrals, obtained by integrating over vertex configurations onℂ\mathbb{C}.
The conditionE=2​V−2E=2V-2ensures that in both constructions the integrand has top-degree on the relevant integration domain.

For the first family, letσ2​n⊆ℝ​ℙ2​n−1\sigma_{2n}\subseteq\mathbb{RP}^{2n-1}denote the real positive coordinate simplex with
homogeneous coordinatesx1,…,x2​n≥0x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n}\geq 0, one for each edge ofGGinduced by the edge-ordering.
For an×nn\times nmatrixXXthe primitive canonical form is defined as the(2​n−1)(2n-1)-form(1)βX2​n−1=tr⁡((X−1​d​X)2​n−1).\beta_{X}^{2n-1}=\operatorname{tr}((X^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X)^{2n-1}).

Associated to the graphGGis its(n−1)×(n−1)(n-1)\times(n-1)Laplacian matrixLGL_{G}whose entries are linear forms inxkx_{k}.
The corresponding canonical integral isIcan​(G)=∫σ2​nβLG2​n−1∈ℝ.I_{\operatorname{can}}(G)=\int_{\sigma_{2n}}\beta_{L_{G}}^{2n-1}\in\mathbb{R}.

These integrals are finite and arise from the study of the stable cohomology of the general linear group[15].
They may also be interpreted as Feynman integrals with massless propagators.

For the second family, consider the configuration space ofn+1n+1pairwise distinct points inℂ\mathbb{C}, one for each vertex ofGG,
modulo translation, rotation and positive real scaling,Zn={z∈ℂn+1∣zi≠zj​for​i≠j}/(ℂ∗⋉ℂ)Z_{n}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\mid z_{i}\neq z_{j}\text{ for }i\neq j\}/(\mathbb{C}^{*}\ltimes\mathbb{C}), which
may be identified with𝔐0,n+2\mathfrak{M}_{0,n+2}the moduli space ofn+2n+2marked points onℙ1\mathbb{P}^{1}.
The corresponding RW-integral isIRW​(G)=1(2​π​i)n−1​∫Zn∑l=12​n∑d≠l2​n(−1)l+d+(l<d)​log⁡(|zl|2)​⋀e≠l,ddlog⁡(|ze|2)∈ℝ,I_{\operatorname{RW}}(G)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{n-1}}\int_{Z_{n}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}\sum_{d\neq l}^{2n}(-1)^{l+d+(l<d)}\log(\left\lvert z_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\operatorname{dlog}(\left\lvert z_{e}\right\rvert^{2})\in\mathbb{R},

whereze=zu−zvz_{e}=z_{u}-z_{v}fore=(u,v)∈EGe=(u,v)\in E_{G},(l<d)(l<d)is11ifl<dl<dand0otherwise,
and the order of the wedge-product is induced by the edge-order of the graph.
These integrals are finite and real valued: they vanish for evennn, while for oddnnthe pre-factor is real.
RW-integrals have been used to study the action of the Grothendieck-Teichmüller group on deformation quantisation[33].
The main theorem of this paper establishes the equality of the two types of integrals:

## Theorem 1.1.IRW​(G)=Ican​(G).I_{\operatorname{RW}}(G)=I_{\operatorname{can}}(G).

From the perspective of Feynman integrals, the canonical integrals may be viewed as the parameter-space representation,
while the RW-integrals correspond to the position-space formulation. The proof makes this correspondence explicit by
constructing an auxiliary integral on the product of position space and parameter
space. Integrating first over the edge-parameters recovers the RW-integral, while
integrating first over position space gives the canonical integral.

## Remark 1.2.

For the sake of clarity in the statements of the theorems,
the definition of the RW-integrals used in this work differs from the original definition in[33]by a factor of(2​π​i)n​22​n−2(2\pi i)^{n}\,2^{2n-2}.

## 1.2.Single-valuedness and weight

Next, we discuss the values of the RW and canonical integrals.
Consider the multiple zeta values (MZVs)ζ​(n1,…,nk):=∑0<j1<j2<…<jk1j1n1​j2n2​…​jknk,ni∈ℕ,nk≥2,\zeta(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}):=\sum_{0<j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{k}}\frac{1}{j_{1}^{n_{1}}j_{2}^{n_{2}}\ldots j_{k}^{n_{k}}},\quad n_{i}\in\mathbb{N},n_{k}\geq 2,

and let𝒵\mathcal{Z}denote theℚ\mathbb{Q}-algebra they generate. This algebra contains a subalgebra𝒵sv\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}of so-called
single-valued multiple zeta values[13].
These numbers arise naturally, for example, in the low-energy expansion of closed string theory[38,39],
and satisfy the same relations as MZVs as well as many more[13]. For example,ζsv​(2​k)=0,ζsv​(2​k+1)=2​ζ​(2​k+1)andζsv​(5,3)=14​ζ​(3)​ζ​(5).\zeta_{\operatorname{sv}}(2k)=0,\quad\zeta_{\operatorname{sv}}(2k+1)=2\zeta(2k+1)\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}\zeta_{\operatorname{sv}}(5,3)=14\zeta(3)\zeta(5).

The weight of a multiple zeta valueζ​(n1,…,nk)\zeta(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})is defined as the sum of its argumentsn1+…+nkn_{1}+\ldots+n_{k}.
We denote by𝒵k\mathcal{Z}_{k}(and𝒵ksv\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}_{k}) theℚ\mathbb{Q}-vector spaces spanned by (single-valued) MZVs of weightkk.
The algebras𝒵\mathcal{Z}and𝒵sv\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}admit decompositions by weight:𝒵=∑k=2∞𝒵k𝒵sv=∑k=2∞𝒵ksv.\mathcal{Z}=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{k}\qquad\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}_{k}.

Conjecturally, there are noℚ\mathbb{Q}-linear relations between (single-valued) MZVs of different weight.
Under this conjecture, the above decompositions are direct sums.

From the definition of the RW-integrals and[9,34,40,4](seeproposition˜3.6) it follows that:

## Proposition 1.3.

LetGGbe a graph withn+1n+1vertices and2​n2nedges, i.e.E=2​V−2E=2V-2.
Then, the RW-integral and thus the canonical integral, evaluates to single-valued
MZVs of weightnn.Ican​(G)=IRW​(G)∈𝒵nsv.I_{\operatorname{can}}(G)=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(G)\in\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}_{n}.

Thus, the three-wheelW3W_{3}, the five wheelW5W_{5}and the zigzagZ5Z_{5},

and any graphG9G_{9}with99loops,1818edges and1010vertices have
canonical integrals of the formIcan​(W3)=c1​ζ​(3),Ican​(W5)=c2​ζ​(5),Ican​(Z5)=c3​ζ​(5),\displaystyle I_{\operatorname{can}}(W_{3})=c_{1}\zeta(3),\quad I_{\operatorname{can}}(W_{5})=c_{2}\zeta(5),\quad I_{\operatorname{can}}(Z_{5})=c_{3}\zeta(5),Ican​(G7)=c4​ζ​(7),Ican​(G9)=c5​ζ​(9)+c6​ζ​(3)3,\displaystyle I_{\operatorname{can}}(G_{7})=c_{4}\zeta(7),\quad I_{\operatorname{can}}(G_{9})=c_{5}\zeta(9)+c_{6}\zeta(3)^{3},

A partial converse ofproposition˜1.3also holds. Rossi and Willwacher show in[33]that the path-ordered exponential
of a specific element in𝔤​𝔯​𝔱1\mathfrak{grt}_{1}, whose coefficients are the RW-integrals, coincides with
the ratio of the KZ and the anti-KZ associator.
Brown shows in[13, Lemma 5.1]that this ratio is the generating series of the single-valued MZVs. This implies:

## Proposition 1.5.

𝒵sv\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}is generated as aℚ\mathbb{Q}-algebra by the RW-integralsIR​W​(G)I_{RW}(G), where we take all graphsGGwithn+1n+1vertices and2​n2nedges,
i.e.E=2​V−2E=2V-2.

However, RW-integrals are not closed under multiplication. For instance,ζ​(3)\zeta(3)andζ​(5)\zeta(5)arise from the three- and five-wheel
graphs, respectively, and have weights three and five.
Their productζ​(3)​ζ​(5)\zeta(3)\zeta(5)which has weight eight, does not correspond to any single RW-integral.
Thus, RW-integrals only form a set of algebra generators for𝒵sv\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}but not a set of vector space generators.

## 1.3.Feynman periods

For every connected finite graphGGone may consider convergent integrals of the form(4)∫σEP​(x)Ψk​Ω​(x),\int_{\sigma_{E}}\frac{P(x)}{\Psi^{k}}\Omega(x),

wherek∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N},P​(x)P(x)is a polynomial in the variablesxx,Ψ\Psiis the first Symanzik polynomial andΩ​(x)\Omega(x)denotes the volume form.
We define theℚ\mathbb{Q}-vector space spanned by all such convergent integrals of all graphs to be the space of Feynman periods𝒫Feyn\mathcal{P}^{\text{Feyn}}.
This space has been studied in detail by Brown in[14].
The role of Feynman periods in the study of Feynman integrals is illustrated by the following example.

## Example 1.6.p2p_{2}p3p_{3}p1p_{1}p4p_{4}ppppG~\widetilde{G}GGFigure 2.The three-wheel graphGGwith external legs on the left, and the two-loop master graphG~\widetilde{G}on the right[5,26].

Consider the three-wheel graph with four external legsGG, shown on the left offigure˜2.
In its scalar Feynman integral in dimensional regularisation,D=4−2​εD=4-2\varepsilon, the Feynman period appears as the pole inε\varepsilon:IG​({mi2},{pi2},D)=13​ε​∫σGΩ​(x)Ψ2+𝒪​(ε0).I_{G}(\{m_{i}^{2}\},\{p_{i}^{2}\},D)=\frac{1}{3\varepsilon}\int_{\sigma_{G}}\frac{\Omega(x)}{\Psi^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{0}).

On the other hand, removing the external legs ofGGand cutting an internal edgeeeyields a two-point graphG~\widetilde{G},
shown on the right offigure˜2.
In the massless case,mi=0m_{i}=0for all internal edges, the corresponding two-point integral has the Feynman period
as the constant term in theε\varepsilon-expansion:IG~​({0},{p2},D)=1p2​∫σGΩ​(x)Ψ2+𝒪​(ε1).I_{\widetilde{G}}(\{0\},\{p^{2}\},D)=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\int_{\sigma_{G}}\frac{\Omega(x)}{\Psi^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1}).

For details and examples see[8,30,2].
NumeratorsP​(x)P(x)arise, for instance, from non-scalar particles, like fermions, or from IBP reductions, and can be interpreted
combinatorially as linear combinations of graphs obtained by subdividing edges by two-valent vertices.

In[15]Brown shows that the canonical integrals are Feynman periods. Together withtheorem˜1.1, this implies:

## Corollary 1.7.

Theℚ\mathbb{Q}-vector space of RW-integrals is contained in𝒫Feyn\mathcal{P}^{\text{Feyn}}.

In general, the structure of𝒫Feyn\mathcal{P}^{\text{Feyn}}is rather delicate and not well understood.
Many multiple zeta values are known to occur, yet not all do. For instance, it is conjectured thatζ​(2)\zeta(2)does not appear[29].
At the same time, Feynman periods are not restricted to multiple zeta values[10].
Moreover, certain simple periods, such aslog⁡(x)\log(x), are known not to appear motivically[14].
Against this background, it is natural to ask which families of values are guaranteed to occur.
We find the following lower bound on the space𝒫Feyn\mathcal{P}^{\text{Feyn}}.

## Theorem 1.8.

Every single-valued multiple zeta value can be realized as aℚ\mathbb{Q}-linear combination of Feynman periods:𝒵sv⊆𝒫Feyn.\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\text{Feyn}}.

## Proof.

Byproposition˜1.5, the algebra𝒵sv\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}is generated overℚ\mathbb{Q}by the RW-integrals. Bycorollary˜1.7, each such integral lies in𝒫Feyn\mathcal{P}^{\text{Feyn}}. It therefore suffices to show that𝒫Feyn\mathcal{P}^{\text{Feyn}}is closed under multiplication. This closure follows from the two-vertex join[8, Proposition 40], or via face maps associated to sub- and quotient graphs[31, Theorem 2].
∎

## Remark 1.9.

It is also known that not all MZVs which appear in𝒫Feyn\mathcal{P}^{\text{Feyn}}are single-valued. For example, the
non-single-valued MZVζ​(12)\zeta(12)occurs[35].

In the study of Feynman integrals, the weight is also an important aspect, and the weight of the RW-integrals,
as described inproposition˜1.3, is rather surprising.

To each graph one can associate a mixed Hodge structure, often referred to as the Feynman motive[6],
such that all integrals of the form as ineq.˜4are periods of this motive.
The associated weight filtration induces a notion of weight on𝒫Feyn\mathcal{P}^{\text{Feyn}}.
For a graph withnnloops, these Hodge structures have weight bounded by4​n−64n-6[6].
Consequently, a generic integral of this type may be expected to evaluate to a period of Hodge weight4​n−64n-6.
If this bound is not attained, the phenomenon is referred to asweight drop[12].

## Example 1.10.

Consider the five wheel graphW5W_{5}withn=5n=5loops,66vertices and1010edges fromfigure˜1.
Its Feynman period and canonical integral[15, Section 10.2]are given by∫σ10Ω​(x)Ψ2=70​ζ​(7)andIcan​(W5)=18​∫σ10(1Ψ2+12​x1​x2​x3​x4​x5Ψ3)​Ω​(x)=1260​ζ​(5).\int_{\sigma_{10}}\frac{\Omega(x)}{\Psi^{2}}=70\zeta(7)\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}I_{\operatorname{can}}(W_{5})=18\int_{\sigma_{10}}\left(\frac{1}{\Psi^{2}}+12\frac{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}x_{5}}{\Psi^{3}}\right)\Omega(x)=1260\zeta(5).

As the Hodge weight corresponds to twice the MZV weight,
the Feynman period70​ζ​(7)70\zeta(7)has Hodge weight1414, as expected for a generic integral.
In contrast, the canonical integral1260​ζ​(5)1260\zeta(5)has Hodge weight1010.

Such a weight drop has been observed in all known computations of canonical integrals[11],
and is unexpected from the viewpoint of Feynman integrals.Proposition˜1.3gives an analytic explanation for this phenomenon.

## 1.4.Cohomology classes of the even graph complex

The result oftheorem˜1.1also has implications for the cohomology of the even graph complex:
The even graph complex is theℚ\mathbb{Q}-vector space𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}spanned
by isomorphism classes of connected, simple, edge-ordered graphs with vertex valency at least33, subject to the relation(G,π∘σ)=sgn⁡(π)⋅(G,σ)forπ∈𝕊E,(G,\pi\circ\sigma)=\operatorname{sgn}(\pi)\cdot(G,\sigma)\mathrel{\quad\text{for}\quad}\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{E},

whereσ\sigmadenotes the edge-ordering ofGGand𝕊E\mathbb{S}_{E}is the symmetric group on{1,…,E}\{1,\ldots,E\}.
The space𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}is bigraded by loop number and degreedeg⁡(G)=2​V−2−E\deg(G)=2V-2-E. The differential is defined as∂G=∑e∈EGG/e,\partial G=\sum_{e\in E_{G}}G/e,

whereG/eG/edenotes the graph obtained fromGGby contracting the edgeee, equipped with the induced orientation.
Since∂2=0\partial^{2}=0,𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}is a complex, and one can consider its homologyH∙​(𝒢​𝒞2)=ker⁡(∂)Im⁡(∂).H_{\bullet}(\mathcal{GC}_{2})=\frac{\operatorname{ker}(\partial)}{\operatorname{Im}(\partial)}.

The homology of𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}is of interest in several areas of mathematics.
In algebraic geometry, it computes a small part of the (stable) cohomology of the moduli stackℳg\mathcal{M}_{g}of smooth curves of genusgg[20].
In number theory, it is closely related to the cohomology of arithmetic groups such asGLn⁡(ℤ)\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z})[15].
From the viewpoint of geometric topology, graph homology is connected to the cohomology ofOut⁡(Fn)\operatorname{Out}(F_{n})[23],
the group of outer automorphisms of the free group onnngenerators.
In addition, it plays a central role in deformation quantisation, where cocycles have been computed explicitly
in order to study Poisson flows[17,18], in operadic deformation theory,
and in the study of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group, where its degree-zero cohomology is identified with the
Grothendieck–Teichmüller Lie algebra[41].
Despite these connections, the homology of𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}remains difficult to compute,
with explicit results known only in very low degrees and for graphs with a small number of loops[42].

The connection to the homology of𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}arises since the integralsIRWI_{\operatorname{RW}}andIcanI_{\operatorname{can}}define functions on the degree-zero part of𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}, that is they are degree-zero cochains on𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}.
Moreover, they are cocycles as they vanish on boundaries i.e.IRW​(∂G)=Ican​(∂G)=0I_{\operatorname{RW}}(\partial G)=I_{\operatorname{can}}(\partial G)=0[33,15].
Hence, they determine cohomology classes[IRW][I_{\operatorname{RW}}]and[Ican][I_{\operatorname{can}}].

Since𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}is also graded by loop order, the homology decomposes as a direct sum.
Thus, each cohomology class splits into components indexed by the loop numberll, which we denote by[IRWl][I^{l}_{\operatorname{RW}}]and[Icanl][I^{l}_{\operatorname{can}}].
Byproposition˜1.3, these classes take values in the space of single-valued multiple zeta values of weightll:[IRWl],[Icanl]∈H0​(𝒢​𝒞2)⊗𝒵lsv.[I^{l}_{\operatorname{RW}}],[I^{l}_{\operatorname{can}}]\in H^{0}(\mathcal{GC}_{2})\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{l}^{\operatorname{sv}}.

Cohomology classes can, for example, be used to detect non-trivial cycles.
Consider the wheel graphsWlW_{l}fromfigure˜1, they define cycles inH0​(𝒢​𝒞2)H_{0}(\mathcal{GC}_{2}),
since contracting any edge produces a multi-edge and hence vanishes.
Their pairing with the cocycleIRWlI^{l}_{\operatorname{RW}}is non-trivial as seen ineq.˜3, which shows that the wheel cyclesWlW_{l}and
the cocyclesIRWl=IcanlI^{l}_{\operatorname{RW}}=I^{l}_{\operatorname{can}}are not exact i.e. they represent non-zero (co)homology classes.

The main result,theorem˜1.1, not only implies that the integrals define the same cohomology class,
but proves the stronger statement:

## Corollary 1.11.

The RW cocycle and the canonical cocycle coincide. That is, the integralsIRWlI_{\operatorname{RW}}^{l}andIcanlI_{\operatorname{can}}^{l}coincide as cocycles.

The cocycles also descend to a quotient of the graph complex.
Recall that a graph has a two-vertex cut if the removal of two vertices disconnects it.
Let𝒢​𝒞2tri\mathcal{GC}_{2}^{\mathrm{tri}}denote the quotient of𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}by the subcomplex spanned by graphs with two-vertex cuts.
Willwacher proved in[43]that𝒢​𝒞2tri\mathcal{GC}_{2}^{\mathrm{tri}}is quasi-isomorphic to𝒢​𝒞2\mathcal{GC}_{2}.

For canonical integrals, the corresponding vanishing on graphs with two-vertex cuts has been shown by Simone Hu in her PhD thesis via a
quite complicated argument. For the RW-integrals, the same vanishing is more direct; seeremark˜3.8. This gives
the following consequence.

## Corollary 1.12.

The cocyclesIRWl=IcanlI_{\operatorname{RW}}^{l}=I_{\operatorname{can}}^{l}descend to the quotient complex𝒢​𝒞2tri\mathcal{GC}_{2}^{\mathrm{tri}}.
In particular, they define cohomology classes[IRWl]=[Icanl]∈H0​(𝒢​𝒞2tri).[I_{\operatorname{RW}}^{l}]=[I_{\operatorname{can}}^{l}]\in H^{0}\bigl(\mathcal{GC}_{2}^{\mathrm{tri}}\bigr).

## 1.5.Effective formulas

The proof oftheorem˜1.1also yields simplified representations of both the RW-integrals and the canonical forms.
Obtaining explicit formulas for canonical forms is difficult as substantial cancellations occur.
Indeed, from the trace formulaβLG2​n−1=tr⁡((LG−1​d​LG)2​n−1)\beta^{2n-1}_{L_{G}}=\operatorname{tr}((L_{G}^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}L_{G})^{2n-1})one would naively expect a denominator of the formΨ2​n−1\Psi^{2n-1}, whereΨ=det(LG)\Psi=\det(L_{G}).
However, Brown shows in[15, Theorem 2.1]that the canonical form can always be written asβLG2​n−1=PG​(x)Ψn+12​Ω​(x),\beta^{2n-1}_{L_{G}}=\frac{P_{G}(x)}{\Psi^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\Omega(x),

for some polynomialPG​(x)P_{G}(x). Thus, roughly three-quarters of the powers ofΨ\Psiexpected in the denominator cancel with the numerator.
In the course of provingtheorem˜1.1, we obtain an alternative explicit expression of the following shape:βLG2​n−1=QG​(x)ΨnΩ​(x)xdwithQG(x)=(2n−1)∑S,T±det(ε~S​d,∙)det(ε~T​d,∙)perm(adj(M)S,T).\displaystyle\beta^{2n-1}_{L_{G}}=\frac{Q_{G}(x)}{\Psi^{n}}\frac{\Omega(x)}{x_{d}}\mathrel{\quad\text{with}\quad}Q_{G}(x)=(2n-1)\sum_{S,T}\pm\det(\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{Sd,\bullet})\det(\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{Td,\bullet})\operatorname{perm}(\operatorname{adj}(M)_{S,T}).

The precise signs, indexing conventions and definitions are given intheorem˜7.1.
Although the expression is initially obtained in the form shown above, the numeratorQG​(x)Q_{G}(x)is divisible byxdx_{d}.
Hence the apparent factorxdx_{d}in the denominator cancels, and the form has denominatorΨn\Psi^{n}.
While this is not the optimal denominator, it nevertheless accounts for approximately two-thirds of the available cancellations.
Moreover, the proof makes the source of these cancellations explicit.
This provides, to our knowledge, the most efficient explicit general formula currently available for computing canonical forms.

Computing canonical forms explicitly is already difficult, but integrating them is even harder.
As discussed insection˜1.3, they arise in the context of Feynman motives,
where complicated periods, such as those associated with aK​3K3surface, may occur[10].
By contrast, RW-integrals are significantly more accessible: their integrands are products of logarithms and dlog-forms,
and explicit integration algorithms and dedicated software are available for such integrals[4,37].
It is therefore useful to simplify the RW-integrals as much as possible.
To this end, we observe that the summations over the distinguished edgesllandddin the definition ofIRWI_{\operatorname{RW}}may be removed:

## Proposition 1.13.

Letl,d∈{1,…,2​n}l,d\in\{1,\ldots,2n\}withl≠dl\neq d. ThenIRW​(G)=(2​n−1)​(−1)l+d+(l<d)(2​π​i)n−1​∫Znlog⁡(|zl|2|zd|2)​⋀e≠l,ddlog⁡(|ze|2|zd|2)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(G)=(2n-1)\frac{(-1)^{l+d+(l<d)}}{(2\pi i)^{n-1}}\int_{Z_{n}}\log\Bigg(\frac{\left\lvert z_{l}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert z_{d}\right\rvert^{2}}\Bigg)\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\operatorname{dlog}\Bigg(\frac{\left\lvert z_{e}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert z_{d}\right\rvert^{2}}\Bigg)

whereze=zu−zvz_{e}=z_{u}-z_{v}for an edgee=(u,v)∈EGe=(u,v)\in E_{G}and(l<d)(l<d)is11ifl<dl<dand0otherwise.

To pass fromZnZ_{n}to an affine chart, one may fix the endpoints of the distinguished edgeede_{d}at0and11.
In this chart,|zd|=1\left\lvert z_{d}\right\rvert=1, the denominators|zd|2\left\lvert z_{d}\right\rvert^{2}disappear.
Thus, after fixing the scale|zd|=1\left\lvert z_{d}\right\rvert=1, the formula reduces the number of terms to be computed from2​n−12n-1to one.
A further promising approach would be to express RW-integrals as the single-valued map applied to realnn-fold integrals,
using the methods introduced in[9]. We plan to investigate this in future work.

## 2.Overview

This work is organised as follows. In the present section, we first extend the definitions of the integralsIRWI_{\operatorname{RW}}andIcanI_{\operatorname{can}}from graphs to matricesA∈ℝ2​n×nA\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times n}.
Next, we outline the main ideas of the proof oftheorem˜1.1and discuss the main technical difficulties.
The remaining sections are organised as follows. Insection˜3,
we establish several properties of the integrals that will be used in the full proof.
Insections˜4,5,6and7, we give complete proofs of the steps outlined below.
Finally, inappendix˜A, we collect a number of standard matrix identities used in the preceding arguments.
Fork∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N}, we denote by[k][k]the ordered set(1,…,k)(1,\ldots,k).

## 2.1.Generalisation to matrices

Letn≥3n\geq 3and consider a real2​n×n2n\times nmatrixAAof ranknn,
such that no row ofAAis equal to0. Denoting the rows ofAAbyaia_{i}, we regard eachaia_{i}as a complex-linear functional onℂn\mathbb{C}^{n},
obtaining2​n2nlinear forms𝒂i:ℂn→ℂ\bm{a}_{i}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}given byz↦𝒂i​(z):=ai​zz\mapsto\bm{a}_{i}(z):=a_{i}z.
For brevity and readability we often write𝒂i\bm{a}_{i}for𝒂i​(z)\bm{a}_{i}(z).
We define the RW-integral associated withAAbyIRW​(A)=(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫CA∑l=12​n(−1)l−1​log⁡(|𝒂l​(z)|2)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e​(z)),I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=\frac{(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{C_{A}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}(-1)^{l-1}\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}(z)\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e}(z)),

where,CA=ConfA⁡(ℂ)/ℝ>0C_{A}=\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}andConfA⁡(ℂ):={z∈ℂn∣𝒂i​(z)≠0​∀i∈[2​n]}\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C}):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mid\bm{a}_{i}(z)\neq 0\;\forall i\in[2n]\}.
Although the integrand involves logarithms and arguments, the resulting integralIRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)is a well-defined single-valued function ofAA.

This definition of the RW-integral appears to differ from the initial definition given insection˜1.
To recoverIRW​(G)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(G)for a given graphGG, we takeAAto be a reduced incidence matrix ofGG, so thatae​(z)=zu−zva_{e}(z)=z_{u}-z_{v}fore=(u,v)∈EGe=(u,v)\in E_{G}.
Passing from the full to the reduced incidence matrix is geometrically equivalent to fixing the removed vertex at0,
which accounts for the translation quotientℂ\mathbb{C}present in the original spaceZnZ_{n}.
Since the generalised spaceCAC_{A}already quotients by positive real scalingℝ>0\mathbb{R}_{>0}, it only remains to account for rotations.
We achieve this by considering the canonical projectionπ:CA→CA/S1\pi:C_{A}\to C_{A}/S^{1}and integrating along theS1S^{1}-fibers.
The resulting integral overCA/S1C_{A}/S^{1}agrees with the original definition ofIRWI_{\operatorname{RW}}, after exchanging the angular differentials with
logarithmic differentials of absolute values; seeproposition˜3.5.

In the graph case, the condition𝒂i​(z)≠0​∀i∈[2​n]\bm{a}_{i}(z)\neq 0\;\forall i\in[2n]reduces tozj≠zkz_{j}\neq z_{k}for all edges(j,k)∈EG(j,k)\in E_{G}.
Taking all possible pairs(j,k)∈[n]2(j,k)\in[n]^{2}withj≠kj\neq k, corresponding to the edges of the complete graphKnK_{n},
yields the classical configuration spaceConfn⁡(ℂ)={z∈ℂn∣zj≠zk​∀j,k∈[n],j≠k}\operatorname{Conf}_{n}(\mathbb{C})=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mid z_{j}\neq z_{k}\;\forall j,k\in[n],j\neq k\}.
The spacesConfA⁡(ℂ)\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C})therefore generalise the classical configuration spaces by allowing arbitrary linear forms𝒂i\bm{a}_{i}, rather than only
forms of the typezj−zkz_{j}-z_{k}.

## Remark 2.1.

Despite the notation,IRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)depends only on the linear forms𝒂1​(z)\bm{a}_{1}(z), …,𝒂2​n​(z)\bm{a}_{2n}(z),
and not on the chosen matrix representation.
Indeed, it is invariant under right multiplication byGLn⁡(ℝ)\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}), which corresponds to a real change of basis ofℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}.
Moreover,IRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)is invariant under rescaling of the individual linear forms: for any diagonal2​n×2​n2n\times 2nmatrixDD, one hasIRW​(D​A)=IRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(DA)=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A).
Thus,IRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)may equivalently be regarded as a function of the associated arrangement of hyperplanesHi=ker⁡(𝒂i​(z))H_{i}=\operatorname{ker}(\bm{a}_{i}(z)).

With the matrixAA, we can also generalise the canonical integrals.
Letσ2​n⊆ℝ​ℙ2​n−1\sigma_{2n}\subseteq\mathbb{RP}^{2n-1}denote the projective simplex with homogeneous coordinatesx=(x1,…,x2​n)x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n}), satisfyingxi≥0x_{i}\geq 0.
We define a mapLA​(x):σ2​n→𝒫n≥0,LA​(x)=AT​diag⁡(x)​AL_{A}(x)\colon\sigma_{2n}\to\mathcal{P}^{\geq 0}_{n},\qquad L_{A}(x)=A^{T}\operatorname{diag}(x)A

from the simplexσ2​n\sigma_{2n}into the space𝒫n≥0\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\geq 0}of positive semidefinite symmetricn×nn\times nmatrices.
We denote its image byσA\sigma_{A}.
This construction generalises the tropical Torelli map[21,19]from the moduli space of tropical curves to the moduli space of tropical abelian varieties.
In the graph case, whenAAis a reduced incidence matrix of a graph with2​n2nedges andn+1n+1vertices, the mapLAL_{A}coincides exactly with
the tropical Torelli map.

The canonical integral associated withAAis then defined byIcan​(A)=∫σAβX2​n−1=∫σ2​nβLA2​n−1.I_{\operatorname{can}}(A)=\int_{\sigma_{A}}\beta^{2n-1}_{X}=\int_{\sigma_{2n}}\beta^{2n-1}_{L_{A}}.

As in the case of the RW-integral,Ican​(A)I_{\operatorname{can}}(A)is invariant under right multiplication ofAAbyGLn⁡(ℝ)\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}).
On the level ofLAL_{A}, this corresponds to the conjugation actionLA↦PT​LA​PL_{A}\mapsto P^{T}L_{A}PforP∈GLn⁡(ℝ)P\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}).
In particular,Ican​(A)I_{\operatorname{can}}(A)depends only on the underlying linear forms𝒂1,…,𝒂2​n\bm{a}_{1},\ldots,\bm{a}_{2n}and not on the specific matrix representationAA.
The definition ofIRWI_{\operatorname{RW}}andIcanI_{\operatorname{can}}can be extended to all2​n×n2n\times n-matricesAAby setting them equal to zero wheneverrank⁡(A)<n\operatorname{rank}(A)<norAAcontains a zero row.

Insection˜4we prove the following generalisation oftheorem˜1.1, which
is recovered by takingAAto be a reduced incidence matrixε~\widetilde{\varepsilon}.

## Theorem 2.2.

Letn≥3n\geq 3andA∈ℝ2​n×nA\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times n}of ranknn, such that no row ofAAis equal to0.
ThenIRW​(A)=Ican​(A).I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=I_{\operatorname{can}}(A).

Although both canonical and RW-integrals are defined for allnn, we show inpropositions˜3.7and3.12that they vanish whenevernnis even.
In the graph case, this corresponds to the integrals vanishing for graphs with an odd number of vertices.

## Remark 2.3.

We note that the canonical integrals admit a further extension to integration of wedge products of formsβX2​n−1\beta_{X}^{2n-1}.
An analogous extension for RW-integrals is currently not known and appears to be more subtle.
One obstruction is that such generalised canonical integrals need not evaluate to single-valued polylogarithms.
For instance, in the case of the complete graphK6K_{6}, the integral ofβLK65∧βLK69\beta^{5}_{L_{K_{6}}}\wedge\beta^{9}_{L_{K_{6}}}involves the multiple zeta valueζ​(3,5)\zeta(3,5)[15],
which is conjecturally not a single-valued multiple zeta value.

In the following, we restrict to the case of oddnn, since the integrals vanish for evennnas discussed above.
Instead of restricting the canonical integrals to integration over subspacesσA\sigma_{A}, we can extend them to
arbitrary locally finite chains on𝒫n≥0/GLn⁡(ℤ)\mathcal{P}^{\geq 0}_{n}/\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z}).
Brown shows in[16]that these integrals are convergent for all such chains. This is because, after
passing to a suitable compactification,
the canonical form extends to the boundary and restricts to zero.
Consequently, it defines a class in the dual of the locally finite (Borel-Moore) homology,[Ican]∈(Hkl​f​(𝒫n/GLn⁡(ℤ);ℝ))∨.[I_{\operatorname{can}}]\in\left(H^{lf}_{k}(\mathcal{P}_{n}/\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\right)^{\vee}.

This space may, via integration pairing, be identified with the compactly supported cohomologyHck​(𝒫n/GLn⁡(ℤ);ℝ)H_{c}^{k}(\mathcal{P}_{n}/\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R}), which in turn is Poincaré dual to the group cohomology ofGLn\operatorname{GL}_{n}:Hdn−k​(GLn⁡(ℤ);ℝ)≅Hdn−k​(𝒫n/GLn⁡(ℤ);ℝ)≅(Hck​(𝒫n/GLn⁡(ℤ);ℝ))∨,H^{d_{n}-k}(\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\cong H^{d_{n}-k}(\mathcal{P}_{n}/\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\cong\left(H_{c}^{k}(\mathcal{P}_{n}/\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R})\right)^{\vee},

wheredn:=n​(n+1)/2d_{n}:=n(n+1)/2.
Thus, the cocycleIcanI_{\operatorname{can}}, is related to the cohomology of the general linear group.

It is natural to ask what values this cocycle takes. To answer this, we use that locally finite homology is isomorphic to the homology
of the Voronoi complex:Hk​(Vorn)≅Hkl​f​(𝒫n/GLn⁡(ℤ);ℝ).H_{k}(\operatorname{Vor}_{n})\cong H^{lf}_{k}(\mathcal{P}_{n}/\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z});\mathbb{R}).

The Voronoi complexVorn\operatorname{Vor}_{n}, as described in[24], is a cell complex whose cells are polyhedral cones in the space𝒫n≥0\mathcal{P}^{\geq 0}_{n}. Importantly, the rays (generators) of the Voronoi cells are rank-one positive semidefinite matrices.
Using simplicial subdivision (triangulation of the link) of these polyhedral cones, we can
reduce the computation of the cocycleIcanI_{\operatorname{can}}to the computation of the integrals for simplicial cones.
Observe then that the imageσA\sigma_{A}of the mapLA​(x)L_{A}(x)is a simplicial cone in𝒫n≥0\mathcal{P}^{\geq 0}_{n}.
Since the rays of the coneσA\sigma_{A}are rank-one matrices, they can be written asaiT​aia_{i}^{T}a_{i}for some vectoraia_{i}.
Hence,σA={x1​a1T​a1+x2​a2T​a2+…+x2​n​a2​nT​a2​n∣(x1,…,x2​n)∈ℝ2​n}.\sigma_{A}=\{x_{1}a_{1}^{T}a_{1}+x_{2}a_{2}^{T}a_{2}+\ldots+x_{2n}a_{2n}^{T}a_{2n}\mid(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\}.

Usingtheorem˜2.2we can compute the cocycleIcanI_{\operatorname{can}}on the Voronoi complex via the RW-integrals.
Since the integration of RW-integrals is much better understood than that of canonical integrals, and arguing similar
to the proof sketch ofproposition˜3.6, we obtain the following description of the values taken by these cocycles.
As the Voronoi complex is rational, the rays can be represented integrally, i.e. withai∈ℤna_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.
We can therefore conclude:

## Corollary 2.4.

The cocycleIcanI_{\operatorname{can}}on the Voronoi complexVorn\operatorname{Vor}_{n}takes values in theℚ\mathbb{Q}-vector space of single-valued polylogarithms
with integer letters (poles), evaluated at integer points.

## 2.2.The idea behind provingtheorem˜2.2

Let us next explain the mechanism behind the proof oftheorem˜2.2. The goal is not to give a full proof, but
to sketch the correspondence between the RW-integral and the canonical
integral which underlies the argument. To keep the mechanism visible in this sketch
we freely interchange limits, derivatives and integrals, ignore convergence issues, and suppress
boundary/counterterm corrections and signs. These issues are addressed in the full proof
insection˜4.

The proof is organised around an auxiliary integralIaux​(A)I_{\operatorname{aux}}(A)on the product
spaceℂn×σ2​n\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}. This integral serves as a bridge between
the two sides: integrating first over the simplexσ2​n\sigma_{2n}produces the
RW-integral, while integrating first overℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}produces the canonical
integral. Thus the equality is proved by realising both integrals as two
different evaluations of the same object. The diagram below summarises this
strategy:Iaux​on​ℂn×σ2​n{I_{\operatorname{aux}}\text{ on }\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}}integral on​ℂn{\text{integral on }\mathbb{C}^{n}}integral on​σ2​n{\text{integral on }\sigma_{2n}}IRW​on​CA{I_{\operatorname{RW}}\text{ on }C_{A}}Ican​on​σ2​n{I_{\operatorname{can}}\text{ on }\sigma_{2n}}∫σ2​n\scriptstyle{\int_{\sigma_{2n}}}Feynman parametrisationGaussian integration∫ℂn\scriptstyle{\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}}scale integrationmatrix identitiesFigure 3.The auxiliary integralIauxI_{\operatorname{aux}}relates the RW and the canonical integral.

Letn≥3n\geq 3, and letd∈[2​n]d\in[2n]. We writez=(z1,…,zn)z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})for the coordinates onℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}.
The simplexσ2​n\sigma_{2n}is initially parametrised by coordinatesα=(α1,…,α2​n)\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{2n}).
Later, when passing from the auxiliary integral to the canonical integral, we change variables by settingαi=xi−1\alpha_{i}=x_{i}^{-1}.
Thus the auxiliary integral is written in the coordinateszzandα\alpha.
The RW-integral is written in thezz-variables, and the canonical integral is written in the dual simplex coordinatesxi=αi−1x_{i}=\alpha_{i}^{-1}.

LetA∈ℝ2​n×nA\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times n}of ranknnsuch that no row ofAAis equal to0. LetL​(α)=AT​diag⁡(α)​AL(\alpha)=A^{T}\operatorname{diag}(\alpha)Aand definePdl:=([c|c]​(diag⁡(∂p)⋅A)l​d,∙(diag⁡(∂p¯)⋅A)l​d,∙ad00ad)andM=(diag⁡(x1,…,x2​n)−AAT0).P_{d}^{l}:=\begin{pmatrix}[c|c](\operatorname{diag}(\partial p)\cdot A)^{ld,\bullet}&(\operatorname{diag}(\partial\overline{p})\cdot A)^{ld,\bullet}\\
\hline\cr a_{d}&0\\
0&a_{d}\end{pmatrix}\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}M=\begin{pmatrix}\operatorname{diag}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n})&-A\\
A^{T}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Here∂p=(∂∂p1,…,∂∂p2​n)\partial p=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{2n}}\right), and(−)l​d,∙(-)^{ld,\bullet}denotes deletion of the rows indexed byllanddd.
The matrixPdlP_{d}^{l}should be understood as a matrix whose entries are differential operators.
Thus, in expressions such asdiag⁡(∂p)⋅A\operatorname{diag}(\partial p)\cdot A, the derivatives do not act on the constant matrixAA.
Rather,AAsupplies the scalar coefficients of the corresponding differential operators.
Explicitly, the(i,j)(i,j)-entry ofdiag⁡(∂p)⋅A\operatorname{diag}(\partial p)\cdot Aisai​j​∂∂pia_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{i}}.

The auxiliary integral is defined asIaux​(A)=1(2​π​i)n​∫ℂn×σ2​n∑l=12​n±limp→0det(Pdl)​(⋀i∈[n]d​zi∧d​zi¯)​Ω​(α)(zT​L​(α)​z¯+pT​A​z+p¯T​A​z¯+αl)2I_{\operatorname{aux}}(A)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}\pm\lim_{p\to 0}\det(P_{d}^{l})\frac{\left(\bigwedge_{i\in[n]}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{i}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{z_{i}}\right)\Omega(\alpha)}{(z^{T}L(\alpha)\overline{z}+p^{T}Az+\overline{p}^{T}A\overline{z}+\alpha_{l})^{2}}

## The RW-integral side

To recover the RW-integral, we must integrate out the simplex variables in the auxiliary integral.
The mechanism governing this step is the Feynman parametrisation.
While typically used (via the Schwinger trick) to rewrite a product of powers as a single simplex integral,
we will use the identity in reverse to evaluate our inner integral. We recall the exact identity:

## Proposition 2.5.

Letk∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N}, letA1,…,Ak∈ℝ>0A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}and lets1,…,sk∈ℝ>0s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}. Then1A1s1​…​Aksk=Γ​(s1+…+sk)Γ​(s1)​…​Γ​(sk)​∫σkα1s1−1​…​αksk−1(α1​A1+…+αk​Ak)s1+…+sk​Ω​(α).\frac{1}{A_{1}^{s_{1}}\ldots A_{k}^{s_{k}}}=\frac{\Gamma(s_{1}+\ldots+s_{k})}{\Gamma(s_{1})\ldots\Gamma(s_{k})}\int_{\sigma_{k}}\frac{\alpha_{1}^{s_{1}-1}\ldots\alpha_{k}^{s_{k}-1}}{(\alpha_{1}A_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{k}A_{k})^{s_{1}+\ldots+s_{k}}}\Omega(\alpha).

To apply this to our specific case, consider the auxiliary integralIaux​(A)I_{\operatorname{aux}}(A). Applying the differential
operatordet(Pdl)\det(P_{d}^{l})and settingp=p¯=0p=\overline{p}=0givesΓ​(2​n)(2​π​i)n​∫ℂn∫σ2​n∑l=12​n±⋀e≠l,d(𝒂e¯​d​𝒂e−𝒂e​d​𝒂e¯)∧d​𝒂d∧d​𝒂d¯(∑eαe​|𝒂e|2+αl)2​n​Ω​(α)\frac{\Gamma(2n)}{(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\int_{\sigma_{2n}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}\pm\frac{\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{e}-\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}})\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{d}}}{(\sum_{e}\alpha_{e}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l})^{2n}}\Omega(\alpha)

Observe that the inner integral over the simplexσ2​n\sigma_{2n}matches the right-hand side of the proposition fork=2​nk=2n,si=1s_{i}=1for1≤i≤2​n1\leq i\leq 2nandAi=|𝒂i|2A_{i}=\left\lvert\bm{a}_{i}\right\rvert^{2}fori≠li\neq landAl=|𝒂l|2+1A_{l}=\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}+1.
By running the Feynman parametrisation backwards, we evaluate this simplex integral giving(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫ℂn∑l=12​n±1|𝒂l|2+1​dlog⁡(|𝒂d|2)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)\frac{(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}\pm\frac{1}{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}+1}\operatorname{dlog}(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})

where we used the identities2​i​darg⁡(𝒂e)=𝒂e¯​d​𝒂e−𝒂e​d​𝒂e¯|𝒂e|2andi​dlog⁡(|𝒂d|2)∧darg⁡(𝒂d)=d​𝒂d∧d​𝒂d¯|𝒂d|22i\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=\frac{\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{e}-\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}}{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvert^{2}}\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}i\operatorname{dlog}(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2})\wedge\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{d})=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{d}}}{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}}

It remains to pass from the integral overℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}to the integral overCAC_{A}. We separate the radial scale using the mapCA×ℝ>0→ℂn∖{0}C_{A}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\{0\}via(z,r)↦r​z(z,r)\mapsto\sqrt{r}z.
Pulling back the preceding integral gives,(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫CA∑l=12​n±(∫0∞1r​|𝒂l|2+1​d​rr)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)\frac{(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{C_{A}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}\pm\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}+1}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}{r}\right)\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})

The radial integral has a finite and a divergent constant part. Formally, this may be written as(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫CA∑l=12​n±log⁡(|𝒂l|2)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)+log⁡(0)​(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫CA∑l=12​n±⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)\frac{(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{C_{A}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}\pm\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})+\log(0)\frac{(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{C_{A}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}\pm\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})

The first term is the RW-integral. The second term is the divergent constant
term. In the full proof it is cancelled by a corresponding counterterm.
At the level of this formal sketch, it vanishes morally as the integrand is invariant under rotation and hence not a volume form.
This route of integration, therefore, givesIaux​(A)=IRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{aux}}(A)=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A).

## The canonical integral side

Next, we computeIauxI_{\operatorname{aux}}in the other order, by integrating first overℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}. This part of the argument has three steps. First, completing the
square turns the integral overℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}into a standard complex Gaussian-type
integral. Second, the differential operatordet(Pdl)\det(P_{d}^{l})is expanded, and
its action on the resulting quadratic form is expressed in terms of permanents.
Third, the determinant-permanent expression which remains is identified with
the canonical form by a purely algebraic matrix identity.

For the first step, we replace the exponent22in the denominator ofIaux​(A)I_{\operatorname{aux}}(A)by a complex parameterss, initially withRe⁡(s)>2​n\operatorname{Re}(s)>2n.
This places the integral in a region of absolute convergence, where the
Gaussian integration overℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}can be performed first.
The resulting expression is then analytically continued back tos=2s=2.
Completing the square in the denominator giveszT​L​z¯+pT​A​z+p¯T​A​z¯+αl=wT​w¯+αl−p¯T​A​L−1​AT​p,z^{T}L\overline{z}+p^{T}Az+\overline{p}^{T}A\overline{z}+\alpha_{l}=w^{T}\overline{w}+\alpha_{l}-\overline{p}^{T}AL^{-1}A^{T}p,

after a change of variables inℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}. The Jacobian of this change of
variables contributes the factordet(L)−1\det(L)^{-1}. Hence the standard complex
Gaussian integral gives(5)Iaux​(A)=∫σ2​n∑l=12​n±1det(L)​Γ​(s−n)Γ​(s)​limp→0det(Pdl)​1(αl−p¯T​A​L−1​AT​p)s​Ω​(α)I_{\operatorname{aux}}(A)=\int_{\sigma_{2n}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}\pm\frac{1}{\det(L)}\frac{\Gamma(s-n)}{\Gamma(s)}\lim_{p\to 0}\det(P_{d}^{l})\frac{1}{(\alpha_{l}-\overline{p}^{T}AL^{-1}A^{T}p)^{s}}\Omega(\alpha)

Next, we expand the differential operatordet(Pdl)\det(P_{d}^{l}). This produces a sum over pairs(S,T)(S,T)partitioning[2​n]∖{l,d}[2n]\setminus\{l,d\}into two sets
of equal cardinality. HereSScorresponds to choosing rows ofPdlP^{l}_{d}giving holomorphic derivatives∂∂pj\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{j}}andTTto choosing anti-holomorphic derivatives∂∂pi¯\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{p_{i}}}.
After the Gaussian integration, thepp-dependence occurs only through the quadratic formp¯T​A​L−1​AT​p\overline{p}^{T}AL^{-1}A^{T}p.
Thus applying thepp- andp¯\overline{p}-derivatives and settingp,p¯=0p,\overline{p}=0amounts to pairing the indices inSSwith the indices inTTin all possible ways.
Since these pairings do not carry alternating signs, the result is a permanent:(6)limp→0∏i∈T∂∂pi¯∏j∈S∂∂pj1(p¯T​A​L−1​AT​p+αl)q−n=(−1)n−1​Γ​(s−1)Γ​(s−n)​αls−1perm(AL−1AT)S,T\lim_{p\to 0}\prod_{i\in T}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{p_{i}}}\prod_{j\in S}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{j}}\frac{1}{(\overline{p}^{T}AL^{-1}A^{T}p+\alpha_{l})^{q-n}}=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}\Gamma(s-1)}{\Gamma(s-n)\alpha_{l}^{s-1}}\operatorname{perm}(AL^{-1}A^{T})_{S,T}

Substituting this identity intoeq.˜5, analytically continuing tos=2s=2, and changing variablesαi↦1/xi\alpha_{i}\mapsto 1/x_{i}, we obtain an expression in terms of minors of the inverse graph matrixM−1M^{-1}.
More precisely, for the index setsS,TS,Tappearing here, one has(A​L−1​AT)S,T=(M−1)S,T(AL^{-1}A^{T})_{S,T}=(M^{-1})_{S,T}.
Thus the expression forIaux​(A)I_{\operatorname{aux}}(A)becomes(7)Iaux​(A)=∫σ2​n∑l=12​n∑S,T∈ℐdl±det((M−1)S​l,T​l)​perm⁡((M−1)S,T)​Ω​(x)xdI_{\operatorname{aux}}(A)=\int_{\sigma_{2n}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}\sum_{S,T\in\mathcal{I}_{d}^{l}}\pm\det((M^{-1})_{Sl,Tl})\operatorname{perm}((M^{-1})_{S,T})\frac{\Omega(x)}{x_{d}}

Hereℐdl={(S,T)∈([2​n]∖{l,d})2∣|S|=|T|=n−1​and​S∩T=∅}\mathcal{I}_{d}^{l}=\{(S,T)\in([2n]\setminus\{l,d\})^{2}\mid\left\lvert S\right\rvert=\left\lvert T\right\rvert=n-1\text{ and }S\cap T=\varnothing\}.

At this stage the analytic part of the argument is finished. What remains is
the algebraic identification of the determinant-permanent expression above

The required combinatorial argument allows us to rewriteeq.˜7as(8)Iaux​(A)=∫σ2​n±Ω​(x)xd​∑τ∈𝕊2​n−1sgn⁡(τ)​Miτ​(1)​iτ​(2)−1​Miτ​(2)​iτ​(3)−1​⋯​Miτ​(2​n−2)​iτ​(2​n−1)−1​Miτ​(2​n−1)​iτ​(1)−1I_{\operatorname{aux}}(A)=\int_{\sigma_{2n}}\pm\frac{\Omega(x)}{x_{d}}\sum_{\tau\in\mathbb{S}_{2n-1}}\operatorname{sgn}(\tau)M^{-1}_{i_{\tau(1)}i_{\tau(2)}}M^{-1}_{i_{\tau(2)}i_{\tau(3)}}\cdots M^{-1}_{i_{\tau(2n-2)}i_{\tau(2n-1)}}M^{-1}_{i_{\tau(2n-1)}i_{\tau(1)}}

It remains to recognise the right-hand side as the canonical integral. For
this, we use the standard trace formula for the canonical form. Since the
canonical form associated toLLagrees with the canonical form associated toMM, it is enough to apply the trace formula toMM. The formtr⁡((M−1​d​M)2​n−1)\operatorname{tr}((M^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}M)^{2n-1})is the trace of the(2​n−1)(2n-1)-st power of the matrix with entriesMi​j−1​d​xjM^{-1}_{ij}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{j}.
Expanding this trace gives a signed sum over cyclic products of the entries ofM−1M^{-1}.
Factoring out the projective volume formΩ​(x)\Omega(x)gives precisely the integrand ofeq.˜8,
and henceIaux​(A)=Ican​(A)I_{\operatorname{aux}}(A)=I_{\operatorname{can}}(A).

## 2.3.Comments on the difficulty of the proof

The preceding argument explains the structure of the proof, but it suppresses
several substantial analytic points. First, the integralIauxI_{\operatorname{aux}}overℂn×σ2​n\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}is not absolutely convergent. In the full
proof, this is handled by truncating the simplex: one replacesσ2​n\sigma_{2n}by the region on which all coordinates satisfyαi>ε\alpha_{i}>\varepsilon, for someε>0\varepsilon>0. The relevant transformations
are then performed at fixedε\varepsilon, and only afterwards at the two ends of the argument, where the resulting
integrals are well defined forε=0\varepsilon=0, is the limitε→0\varepsilon\to 0taken.

Furthermore, for the scale integration on the RW-integral side to be well defined, one must introduce a counterterm in the auxiliary integral.
This counterterm is obtained by replacingllwithdd, while in both the original term and the counterterm the sum overllis restricted tol≠dl\neq d.

Finally, one has to justify the repeated interchange of limits, derivatives
and integrals. These technical points account for a significant part of the
length of the full proof.

## 2.4.Notation

Before we come to the main sections of this work we need to introduce some notations.
Unless stated otherwise,xx,α\alpha, andzzdenote vectors of coordinates[x1:…:xn]∈ℝℙn−1,[α1:…:αn]∈ℝℙn−1andz1,…,zn∈ℂn[x_{1}:\ldots:x_{n}]\in\mathbb{RP}^{n-1},\quad[\alpha_{1}:\ldots:\alpha_{n}]\in\mathbb{RP}^{n-1}\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}

for some fixedn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}.
For homogeneous coordinates ofℝ​ℙn−1\mathbb{RP}^{n-1}(such asxxandα\alpha) and for complex coordinatesz∈ℂnz\in\mathbb{C}^{n}, we define
the (projective) volume formsΩ​(x)=∑i=1n(−1)i−1​xi​d​x1∧…∧d​xi^∧…∧d​xnandΩ​(z)=⋀i=1nd​z∧d​z¯.\Omega(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i-1}x_{i}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{n}\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}\Omega(z)=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{z}.

We denote bydiag⁡(x)\operatorname{diag}(x)ordiag⁡(x1,…,xn)\operatorname{diag}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})the diagonal matrix associated to the elementsx1,…,xnx_{1},\ldots,x_{n}.
The permanent of an×nn\times nmatrixA=(ai​j)A=(a_{ij})is defined byperm⁡(A)=∑σ∈𝕊na1​σ​(1)​…​ak​σ​(k).\operatorname{perm}(A)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{n}}a_{1\sigma(1)}\ldots a_{k\sigma(k)}.

where𝕊n\mathbb{S}_{n}denotes the symmetric group onnnelements.
The adjugate of an invertible matrixAA, that is the matrixdet(A)⋅A−1\det(A)\cdot A^{-1}, is denoted byadj⁡(A)\operatorname{adj}(A).

We write[n][n]for the ordered set(1,…,n)(1,\ldots,n). For two ordered setsS=(s1,…,sk)S=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})andT=(t1,…,tl)T=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l}), we
define their concatenation byS​T:=(s1,…,sk,t1,…,tl).ST:=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k},t_{1},\ldots,t_{l}).

By a slight abuse of notation, we writeS​dSdinstead ofS​(d)S(d)when appending a single elementdd.
For an ordered setSS, we denote byS∖{si1,…,sij}S\setminus\{s_{i_{1}},\ldots,s_{i_{j}}\}the ordered subset obtained by removing
the indicated elements, with the induced ordering inherited fromSS.

Since the elements ofS,T⊆ℕS,T\subseteq\mathbb{N}are themselves ordered,
each such set carries two natural orders: the given order and the standard increasing order onℕ\mathbb{N}.
For an ordered subsetA⊆ℕA\subseteq\mathbb{N}of cardinalitynn, letπ∈𝕊n\pi\in\mathbb{S}_{n}be the permutation that reorders the elements ofAAin
increasing order. We definesgn⁡(A):=sgn⁡(π)\operatorname{sgn}(A):=\operatorname{sgn}(\pi).
Forx∈Ax\in A, we writeA<x:={a∈A∣a<x}A_{<x}:=\{a\in A\mid a<x\}for the set of elements ofAAsmaller thanxx.
Fora,b∈[n]a,b\in[n]we also use the notation(a<b)(a<b)for the indicator function, which equals11ifa<ba<band0otherwise.

LetBBbe am×nm\times nmatrix and letS⊆[m],T⊆[n]S\subseteq[m],T\subseteq[n]be ordered subsets.
We defineBS,T=(bi​j)i∈S,j∈TandBS,T=(bi​j)i∈[m]∖S,j∈[n]∖TB_{S,T}=(b_{ij})_{i\in S,j\in T}\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}B^{S,T}=(b_{ij})_{i\in[m]\setminus S,j\in[n]\setminus T}

Thus,BS,TB_{S,T}denotes the submatrix ofBBwhose rows are indexed bySSand whose columns are indexed byTT, taken in their prescribed orders.
Similarly,BS,TB^{S,T}denotes the submatrix obtained by deleting the rows indexed bySSand columns indexed byTT.
The notationBS,TB_{S,T}is intended to generalise the entry notationbi​jb_{ij}: the subscripts indicate the rows and columns that determine the submatrix,
while the superscripts inBS,TB^{S,T}indicate the rows and columns that are omitted.

To indicate selection or removal of only rows or only columns, we use a placeholder symbol∙\bullet. Specifically,BS,∙=(bi​j)i∈S,j∈[n]B∙,T=(bi​j)i∈[m],j∈TBS,∙=(bi​j)i∈[m]∖S,j∈[n]B∙,T=(bi​j)i∈[m],j∈[n]∖T.\begin{array}[]{ll}B_{S,\bullet}=(b_{ij})_{i\in S,j\in[n]}\qquad&B_{\bullet,T}=(b_{ij})_{i\in[m],j\in T}\\
B^{S,\bullet}=(b_{ij})_{i\in[m]\setminus S,j\in[n]}\qquad&B^{\bullet,T}=(b_{ij})_{i\in[m],j\in[n]\setminus T}.\end{array}

## 3.Properties of canonical and RW-Integrals

## 3.1.RW-Integrals

In the overview we defined the generalised RW-Integrals as follows:

## Definition 3.1.

Letn≥3n\geq 3and consider a real2​n×n2n\times nmatrixA∈ℝ2​n×nA\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times n}of ranknn,
such that no row ofAAis equal to0. Denoting the rows ofAAbyaia_{i}, we obtain2​n2nlinear forms𝒂i:ℂn→ℂ\bm{a}_{i}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}given byz↦𝒂i​(z):=ai​zz\mapsto\bm{a}_{i}(z):=a_{i}z.
We define the RW-integral associated withAAbyIRW​(A)=(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫CA∑l=12​n(−1)l−1​log⁡(|𝒂l​(z)|2)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e​(z)),I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=\frac{(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{C_{A}}\sum_{l=1}^{2n}(-1)^{l-1}\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}(z)\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e}(z)),

where,CA=ConfA⁡(ℂ)/ℝ>0C_{A}=\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C})/\mathbb{R}_{>0}andConfA⁡(ℂ):={z∈ℂn∣𝒂i​(z)≠0​∀i∈{1,…,2​n}}\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C}):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mid\bm{a}_{i}(z)\neq 0\;\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,2n\}\}.
We equipCAC_{A}with the orientation induced fromιR​Ω​(z)\iota_{R}\Omega(z), whereΩ​(z)\Omega(z)is the standard volume form onℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}andRRis the radial vector fieldR=∑i=1nzi​∂∂zi+zi¯​∂∂zi¯.R=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}+\overline{z_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z_{i}}}.

SinceιR​Ω\iota_{R}\Omegais rescaled by a positive function under theℝ>0\mathbb{R}_{>0}action,
it induces a well-defined orientation on the quotientCAC_{A}.
Thus fixing our convention for integration.

To show that the integral is indeed well-defined we need to show that the integrand is defined onCAC_{A}, that
is that it is scale invariant. For this we need the following lemma:

## Lemma 3.2.

The differential formω=∑l=12​n(−1)l−1​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝐚e)\omega=\sum_{l=1}^{2n}(-1)^{l-1}\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})onConfA⁡(ℂ)\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C})vanishes identically.

## Proof.

Define the vector fieldXXand the top-degree formα\alphaonConfA⁡(ℂ)\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C})asX=i​∑i=1nzi​∂∂zi−zi¯​∂∂zi¯,α=⋀edarg⁡(𝒂e).X=i\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}-\overline{z_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z_{i}}},\qquad\alpha=\bigwedge_{e}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e}).

whereXXis the vector field generating the diagonalS1S^{1}-action.
ThenιX​darg⁡(𝒂e)=1\iota_{X}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=1and thusω=±ιX​α\omega=\pm\iota_{X}\alpha.

Now letRRbe the vector field generating real scaling. Sincedarg⁡(𝒂e)\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})is invariant under real scaling,
it follows thatιR​α=0\iota_{R}\alpha=0.
SinceRRis nowhere vanishing andα\alphahas top degree onConfA⁡(ℂ)\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C}), contraction withRRis injective on top-degree forms. Thereforeα=0\alpha=0, and soω=±ιX​α=0\omega=\pm\iota_{X}\alpha=0.
∎

This allows us to show the scale invariance:

## Corollary 3.3.

Letλ∈ℝ>0\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}and letΦλ​(z):CA→CA,z↦λ​z\Phi_{\lambda}(z)\colon C_{A}\to C_{A},z\mapsto\lambda zbe the scaling action,
thenΦλ∗​ωRW​(A)=ωRW​(A).\Phi_{\lambda}^{*}\omega_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=\omega_{\operatorname{RW}}(A).

## Proof.

Using the fact thatdarg⁡(λ​𝒂e)=darg⁡(𝒂e)\operatorname{darg}(\lambda\bm{a}_{e})=\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})andlemma˜3.2we calculate:Φλ∗​ωRW​(A)\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda}^{*}\omega_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=∑l=12​n(−1)l−1​log⁡(λ2​|𝒂l|2)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{2n}(-1)^{l-1}\log(\lambda^{2}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=ωRW​(A)+log⁡(λ2)​∑l=12​n(−1)l−1​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)=ωRW​(A),\displaystyle=\omega_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)+\log(\lambda^{2})\sum_{l=1}^{2n}(-1)^{l-1}\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=\omega_{\operatorname{RW}}(A),

which proves the corollary.
∎

## Remark 3.4(Invariance properties).

The RW-integralsIRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)enjoy several natural invariance properties:
- (1)

RightGLn⁡(ℝ)\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})-invariance: forP∈GLn⁡(ℝ)P\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}), we haveIRW​(A​P)=IRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(AP)=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A).
Indeed, writingω​(A)\omega(A)for the integrand indefinition˜3.1, we computeIRW​(A​P)=∫CA​Pω​(A​P)​(z)=∫CA​Pω​(A)​(P​z)=∫CAω​(A)​(y)=IRW​(A).I_{\operatorname{RW}}(AP)=\int_{C_{AP}}\omega(AP)(z)=\int_{C_{AP}}\omega(A)(Pz)=\int_{C_{A}}\omega(A)(y)=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A).

where we used the change of variablesy=P​zy=Pzand(ai​P)​z=ai​y(a_{i}P)z=a_{i}y.
- (2)

Dependence on the ordering: the value ofIRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)depends on the ordering of the rows ofAAonly up to a sign,
corresponding to the permutation of differential forms in the wedge product.
- (3)

Row rescaling: the integral is invariant under rescaling of the rows ofAA, i.e. forDDa diagonal2​n×2​n2n\times 2nmatrix,
we haveIRW​(D​A)=IRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(DA)=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A).
Indeed, letd1,…,d2​nd_{1},\ldots,d_{2n}be the diagonal entries ofDD. ThenCD​A=CAC_{DA}=C_{A}, as the conditions𝒂i​(z)=0\bm{a}_{i}(z)=0are invariant
under rescaling. Since(D​A)i​(z)=di​𝒂i​(z)(DA)_{i}(z)=d_{i}\bm{a}_{i}(z)we havelog⁡(|(D​A)l|2)=log⁡(|dl|2)+log⁡(|𝒂l|2)\log(\left\lvert(DA)_{l}\right\rvert^{2})=\log(\left\lvert d_{l}\right\rvert^{2})+\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2})anddarg⁡((D​A)i​(z))=darg⁡(𝒂i​(z))\operatorname{darg}((DA)_{i}(z))=\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{i}(z)). Hence,IRW​(D​A)=IRW​(A)+∑l=12​n(−1)l−1​log⁡(|dl|2)​∫CA⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)=IRW​(A).I_{\operatorname{RW}}(DA)=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)+\sum_{l=1}^{2n}(-1)^{l-1}\log(\left\lvert d_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\int_{C_{A}}\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A).

Here the additional terms vanish by the Kontsevich vanishing lemma[28, Section 6.6.1].

Next, we show that the RW-integral as defined insections˜1and3.1agree.
The representation fromsection˜1is often more suited
for explicit computations and for establishing vanishing properties, whereas the form ofdefinition˜3.1is slightly more natural
and needed for the proof oftheorem˜2.2.

l\neq d\end{subarray}}(-1)^{l+d+(l<d)}\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\operatorname{dlog}(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvert^{2}),

## Proof.

Recall the formula forIRWI_{\operatorname{RW}}as indefinition˜3.1.
Letπ:CA→CA/S1\pi:C_{A}\to C_{A}/S^{1}denote the canonical projection.
SinceCA/S1C_{A}/S^{1}is canonically isomorphic toZA=ConfA/ℂ∗Z_{A}=\operatorname{Conf}_{A}/\mathbb{C}^{*}, andπ\piis a trivialS1S^{1}-bundle,
we may integrate along the fibres. l\neq d\end{subarray}}(-1)^{l+d+(l<d)}\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e}).

where we used absolute convergence (theorem˜5.1) to justify the integration along the fibre.

Integratingd​φ\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphiover the fibre yields a factor of2​π2\pi, and hence(9)IRW​(A)=(2​i)2​n−2(2​π​i)n−1​∫ZA∑l,dl≠d(−1)l+d+(l<d)​log⁡(|𝒂l|2)​⋀e≠l,ddarg⁡(𝒂e).I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=\frac{(2i)^{2n-2}}{(2\pi i)^{n-1}}\int_{Z_{A}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l,d\\
l\neq d\end{subarray}}(-1)^{l+d+(l<d)}\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e}).

To replace thedarg\operatorname{darg}-forms bydlog\operatorname{dlog}-forms, we use a standard trick due to Kontsevich[28, Section 6.6.1]:⋀e≠l,ddarg⁡(𝒂e)\displaystyle\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=1(2​i)2​n−2​⋀e≠l,d(dlog⁡(𝒂e)−dlog⁡(𝒂e¯))\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2i)^{2n-2}}\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\left(\operatorname{dlog}(\bm{a}_{e})-\operatorname{dlog}(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}})\right)=(−1)n−1(2​i)2​n−2​⋀e≠l,d(dlog⁡(𝒂e)+dlog⁡(𝒂e¯))=(−1)n−1(2​i)2​n−2​⋀e≠l,dlog⁡(|𝒂e|2).\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{(2i)^{2n-2}}\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\left(\operatorname{dlog}(\bm{a}_{e})+\operatorname{dlog}(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}})\right)=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{(2i)^{2n-2}}\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvert^{2}).

In the second equality, we use that only terms withn−1n-1holomorphic andn−1n-1anti-holomorphic differentials contribute,
as we are considering a top-degree form on an(n−1)(n-1)-dimensional complex manifold.

Substituting this identity intoeq.˜9yields the desired expression and completes the proof.

We will now sketch the proof of the weight and values the RW-integrals take in the case of graphs, as explained insection˜1.2, that is:

## Proposition 3.6.

LetGGbe a graph withn+1n+1vertices and2​n2nedges. Then, the RW-integral and thus the canonical integral, evaluates to single-valued
MZVs of weightnn.Ican​(G)=IRW​(G)∈𝒵nsv.I_{\operatorname{can}}(G)=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(G)\in\mathcal{Z}^{\operatorname{sv}}_{n}.

The proof sketch is based on the theory of[40,4].

## Sketch of proof.

LetXk={0,1,z2,…,zk}X_{k}=\{0,1,z_{2},\ldots,z_{k}\}denote both a set of points inℂ\mathbb{C}and the corresponding alphabet.
We writeConfk⁡(ℂ)={z∈ℂk−1∣zi−zj≠0​for​zi,zj∈Xk}\operatorname{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{C})=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{k-1}\mid z_{i}-z_{j}\neq 0\text{ for }z_{i},z_{j}\in X_{k}\}for the configuration space.
Let𝒜d​(Xk)\mathcal{A}^{d}(X_{k})be theℚ\mathbb{Q}-vector space of single-valueddd-forms onConfk⁡(ℂ)\operatorname{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{C})generated by wedges
ofdlog⁡(zi−zj)\operatorname{dlog}(z_{i}-z_{j})anddlog⁡(zi¯−zj¯)\operatorname{dlog}(\overline{z_{i}}-\overline{z_{j}})forzi,zj∈Xkz_{i},z_{j}\in X_{k}.

Let𝒱w​(Xk)\mathcal{V}_{w}(X_{k})be theℚ\mathbb{Q}-vector space of single-valued polylogarithms of weightww[7]. Is is
generated by productsℒv1​(a1)​…​ℒvr​(ar)\mathcal{L}_{v_{1}}(a_{1})\ldots\mathcal{L}_{v_{r}}(a_{r})whereviv_{i}is a word in the alphabetXkX_{k},ai∈Xka_{i}\in X_{k},
and the weight is the sum of the word lengths.
We set𝒰wd:=𝒜d⊗𝒱w\mathcal{U}_{w}^{d}:=\mathcal{A}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{w}.

The key property is that these spaces are stable under integration in the following sense:
Ifω∈𝒰w2​k​(Xk)\omega\in\mathcal{U}_{w}^{2k}(X_{k})is a top-degree form, then there exists a primitiveα∈𝒰w2​k−1​(Xk)\alpha\in\mathcal{U}_{w}^{2k-1}(X_{k}),
withd​ω=α\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega=\alpha, which has bidegree(0,1)(0,1)ind​zk∧d​zk¯\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{k}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{z_{k}}.
Schnetz’s residue theorem[36]gives:12​π​i​∫zk∈ℂ∖Xk−1ω=∑j=1k−1Reszk¯=zj¯α∈𝒰w+12​k−2​(Xk−1).\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{z_{k}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus X_{k-1}}\omega=\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\operatorname*{Res}_{\overline{z_{k}}=\overline{z_{j}}}\alpha\in\mathcal{U}_{w+1}^{2k-2}(X_{k-1}).

Thus integrating out one complex variable lowers the degree of the form by22and raises the polylogarithmic weight by11.

We now apply this to the RW-integral representation fromproposition˜3.5,
where we have identifiedZAZ_{A}withℂn−1\mathbb{C}^{n-1}by fixingz1=1z_{1}=1.
The integrand lies in𝒰12​n−2​(Xn)\mathcal{U}_{1}^{2n-2}(X_{n}), sincelog⁡(|zi−zj|2)=ℒzj​(zi)−ℒ0​(zj)\log(\left\lvert z_{i}-z_{j}\right\rvert^{2})=\mathcal{L}_{z_{j}}(z_{i})-\mathcal{L}_{0}(z_{j})is a single-valued
hyperlogarithm of weight11.

Integrating successively the variableszn,zn−1,…,z2z_{n},z_{n-1},\ldots,z_{2}, using the primitive and residue formula at each step gives
a sequence of integrands in the following spaces𝒰12​n−2​(Xn)⟶𝒰22​n−4​(Xn−1)⟶⋯⟶𝒰n0​(X1).\mathcal{U}^{2n-2}_{1}(X_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}^{2n-4}_{2}(X_{n-1})\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}^{0}_{n}(X_{1}).

The final result is therefore a single-valued polylogarithm of weightnnin the alphabet{0,1}\{0,1\}, evaluated at0or11.
Since the evaluations at0vanish, the remaining terms are single-valued polylogarithms in the alphabet{0,1}\{0,1\}evaluated at11.
These are precisely the single-valued MZVs. Hence the RW-integral lies in the space of single-valued MZVs of weightnn.
∎

Finally, we record three vanishing properties of the RW-Integrals:

## Proposition 3.7.

The RW-integralIR​W​(A)I_{RW}(A)vanishes whenever one of the following conditions holds:
- a)

nnis even,
- b)

there existsi∈[2​n]i\in[2n]such thatrank⁡(Ai,∙)<n\operatorname{rank}(A^{i,\bullet})<n.
- c)

there existsj∈[n]j\in[n]such thatA∙,jA^{\bullet,j}is in block-diagonal form after a suitable permutation of rows and columns.

## Proof.

We first prove a).
Complex conjugation changes the orientation of the(n−1)(n-1)-dimensional complex manifoldZA=ConfA⁡(ℂ)/ℂ∗Z_{A}=\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C})/\mathbb{C}^{*}by(−1)n−1(-1)^{n-1}, while leaving the integrand invariant. Hence,IRW​(A)=(−1)n−1​IRW​(A),I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=(-1)^{n-1}I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A),

and a) follows trivially.

We now prove b). Suppose thatrank⁡(Ai,∙)<n\operatorname{rank}(A^{i,\bullet})<n. Then the rowsa1,…,ai−1,ai+1,…,a2​na_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{2n}span a vector space of dimension at mostn−1n-1. Consequently, there exists a matrixP∈GLn⁡(ℝ)P\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})such that the first column ofAi,∙​PA^{i,\bullet}Pvanishes identically. DefineA~:=A​P\widetilde{A}:=AP.

By theGLn\operatorname{GL}_{n}-invariance of the RW-integral (remark˜3.4), we haveIRW​(A~)=IRW​(A)I_{\operatorname{RW}}(\widetilde{A})=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A).
Take the form of the RW-integral fromproposition˜3.5, and
identifyZA~Z_{\widetilde{A}}withYA~:={z∈ℂn−1∣𝒂~i​(z,1)≠0​∀i∈[2​n]}Y_{\widetilde{A}}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n-1}\mid\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{i}(z,1)\neq 0\;\forall i\in[2n]\}by fixing the affine chartzn=1z_{n}=1. We obtainIRW​(A)=(−1)n−1(2​π​i)n−1​∫YA~∑l,dl≠d(−1)l+d+(l<d)​log⁡(|𝒂~l​(z,1)|2)​⋀e≠l,ddlog⁡(|𝒂~e​(z,1)|2).I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{(2\pi i)^{n-1}}\int_{Y_{\widetilde{A}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l,d\\
l\neq d\end{subarray}}(-1)^{l+d+(l<d)}\log(\left\lvert\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{l}(z,1)\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\operatorname{dlog}(\left\lvert\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{e}(z,1)\right\rvert^{2}).

By construction, the first coordinate appears only in the linear forma~​(z,1)\widetilde{a}(z,1). Hence, among all differential formsdlog⁡(|𝒂~e​(z,1)|2)\operatorname{dlog}(\left\lvert\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{e}(z,1)\right\rvert^{2}), only the term corresponding toe=ie=ican containd​z1\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{1}ord​z1¯\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{z_{1}}.
Therefore, any term appearing in the integrand contains at most one ofd​z1\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{1}andd​z1¯\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{z_{1}}, but never both.
Since a nonzero top-degree form on the complex manifoldYA~⊆ℂn−1Y_{\widetilde{A}}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n-1}must contain both,
the integrand vanishes identically.

Finally, we prove c). By multiplyingAAon the right by a suitable permutation matrixP∈GLn⁡(ℝ)P\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}),
we may assume that thenn-th column is the one whose removal yields a block-diagonal matrix.
Thus, we may writeA=(U0∗0V∗).A=\begin{pmatrix}U&0&*\\
0&V&*\end{pmatrix}.

whereU∈ℝi×jU\in\mathbb{R}^{i\times j}andV∈ℝ(2​n−i)×(n−1−j)V\in\mathbb{R}^{(2n-i)\times(n-1-j)}.
In particular, the linear forms𝒖k:ℂj→ℂ\bm{u}_{k}:\mathbb{C}^{j}\to\mathbb{C}corresponding to the rows ofUUdepend only on the variablesz1,…,zjz_{1},\ldots,z_{j},
while the linear forms𝒗k:ℂn−1−j→ℂ\bm{v}_{k}:\mathbb{C}^{n-1-j}\to\mathbb{C}corresponding
to the rows ofVVdepend only onzj+1,…,zn−1z_{j+1},\ldots,z_{n-1}.

Using the form of the RW-integral fromproposition˜3.5and identifyingZAZ_{A}with the affine chartYAY_{A}as in the proof of b), we see thatYAY_{A}decomposes as a productYA≅XU×XVY_{A}\cong X_{U}\times X_{V}whereXU\displaystyle X_{U}:={z∈ℂj∣𝒖k​(z)≠0​∀k∈{1,…,i}}\displaystyle:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{j}\mid\bm{u}_{k}(z)\neq 0\;\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,i\}\}XV\displaystyle X_{V}:={z∈ℂn−1−j∣𝒗k​(z,1)≠0​∀k∈{i+1,…,2​n}}.\displaystyle:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n-1-j}\mid\bm{v}_{k}(z,1)\neq 0\;\forall k\in\{i+1,\ldots,2n\}\}.

Consequently, the integrand splits into a product of a form depending only on the variables forUU(z1,…,zjz_{1},\ldots,z_{j}), and a form
depending only on the variables forVV(zj+1,…,zn−1z_{j+1},\ldots,z_{n-1}), that isIRW​(A)\displaystyle I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=∑d=12​n∑l=1l≠di±(∫XUlog⁡(|𝒂l|2)​⋀e=1e≠l,didarg⁡(𝒂e))​(∫XV⋀e=i+1e≠d2​ndarg⁡(𝒂e))\displaystyle=\sum_{d=1}^{2n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=1\\
l\neq d\end{subarray}}^{i}\pm\Bigg(\int_{X_{U}}\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}e=1\\
e\neq l,d\end{subarray}}^{i}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})\Bigg)\Bigg(\int_{X_{V}}\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}e=i+1\\
e\neq d\end{subarray}}^{2n}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})\Bigg)+∑d=12​n∑l=il≠d2​n±(∫XU⋀e=1e≠didarg⁡(𝒂e))​(∫XVlog⁡(|𝒂l|2)​⋀e=i+1e≠l,d2​ndarg⁡(𝒂e)).\displaystyle+\sum_{d=1}^{2n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=i\\
l\neq d\end{subarray}}^{2n}\pm\Bigg(\int_{X_{U}}\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}e=1\\
e\neq d\end{subarray}}^{i}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})\Bigg)\Bigg(\int_{X_{V}}\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}e=i+1\\
e\neq l,d\end{subarray}}^{2n}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})\Bigg).

By the Kontsevich vanishing lemma[28, Section 6.6.1]it follows that the integral overXVX_{V}in the first sum, and the integral
