## Remark 3.8.

In the case of the RW-integral associated to a graphGG, c) implies that the integral vanishes wheneverGGcontains a two-vertex cut.
This also follows from[33, Lemma 7.5].

Indeed, letGGbe a graph with a two-vertex cut given by verticesuu,vv. Choose an incidence matrixε\varepsilonofGGsuch that the firstkkedges belong to one connected component ofG∖{u,v}G\setminus\{u,v\}and the remaining edges belong to the other component.
Moreover, assume that the columns corresponding touuandvvare the first and second columns ofε\varepsilon, respectively.

Now consider the reduced incidence matrixε~=ε∙,1\widetilde{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{\bullet,1}.
Then the RW-integral ofGGis given by the RW-integral associated toA=ε~A=\widetilde{\varepsilon}.
Since the firstkkrows ofε\varepsiloncorrespond to
one connected component ofG∖{u,v}G\setminus\{u,v\}, and the remaining rows correspond to the other, it follows thatε~∙,1\widetilde{\varepsilon}^{\bullet,1}has block-diagonal form. Hence, byproposition˜3.7c), we conclude thatIRW​(G)=IRW​(ε~)=0I_{\operatorname{RW}}(G)=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(\widetilde{\varepsilon})=0.

## 3.2.Canonical Integrals

In the overview we defined the canonical integral as follows:

## Definition 3.9.

Letn≥3n\geq 3, and letA∈ℝ2​n×nA\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times n}of ranknn, such that no row ofAAis equal to0.
Further, letσ2​n⊆ℝ​ℙ2​n−1\sigma_{2n}\subseteq\mathbb{RP}^{2n-1}denote the simplex with homogeneous coordinatesx=(x1,…,x2​n)x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n}), satisfyingxi≥0x_{i}\geq 0.LA​(x):σ2​n→𝒫n≥0L_{A}(x):\sigma_{2n}\to\mathcal{P}^{\geq 0}_{n}given byLA​(x)=AT​diag⁡(x)​AL_{A}(x)=A^{T}\operatorname{diag}(x)Afrom the simplexσ2​n\sigma_{2n}into the space𝒫n≥0\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\geq 0}of positive semidefinite symmetricn×nn\times nmatrices.
The canonical integral and the canonical form are defined byIcan​(A)=∫σ2​nβL2​n−1andβL2​n−1=tr⁡((L−1​d​L)2​n−1).I_{\operatorname{can}}(A)=\int_{\sigma_{2n}}\beta^{2n-1}_{L}\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}\beta^{2n-1}_{L}=\operatorname{tr}((L^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}L)^{2n-1}).

We orientσ2​n\sigma_{2n}by requiring that the projective volume formΩ​(x)\Omega(x)evaluates positively
on the ordered frame∂2∧…∧∂2​n\partial_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge\partial_{2n}.

## Remark 3.10.

The canonical forms are invariant under the right action ofGLn⁡(ℝ)\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})onAA, given byA↦A​PA\mapsto APforP∈GLn⁡(ℝ)P\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}).
On the level ofLL, this corresponds to invariance under conjugationL↦PT​L​PL\mapsto P^{T}LP.
In fact, this invariance characterises the canonical forms as the unique primitiveGLn⁡(ℝ)\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})-invariant differential forms up to scalar.

Moreover, the same canonical forms arise from different matrix representations associated to graphs.
For example, ifMMdenotes the graph matrix fromdefinition˜A.1, then[15, Prop. 6.23]shows thatβL2​n−1=βM2​n−1\beta^{2n-1}_{L}=\beta^{2n-1}_{M}.

The expression for the canonical form as the trace of a high power ofL−1​d​LL^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}Lis not particularly well suited for explicit computations,
since the variables and differentials are heavily intertwined. Using Dodgson polynomials (seeappendix˜A),
one can instead derive a representation as a polynomial times a volume form:

## Proposition 3.11.

Letd∈[2​n]d\in[2n]and letId=(i1,…,i2​n−1)=I∖{d}I_{d}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2n-1})=I\setminus\{d\}with the induced ordering.
Then the canonical form can be written asβL2​n−1=(−1)d+1​fd​(x)xd​Ω​(x).\beta_{L}^{2n-1}=(-1)^{d+1}\frac{f_{d}(x)}{x_{d}}\Omega(x).

withfd​(x)=1Ψ2​n−1​∑τ∈𝕊2​n−1sgn⁡(τ)​Ψiτ​(1),iτ​(2)​Ψiτ​(2),iτ​(3)​…​Ψiτ​(2​n−1),iτ​(1).f_{d}(x)=\frac{1}{\Psi^{2n-1}}\sum_{\tau\in\mathbb{S}_{2n-1}}\operatorname{sgn}(\tau)\Psi^{i_{\tau(1)},i_{\tau(2)}}\Psi^{i_{\tau(2)},i_{\tau(3)}}\ldots\Psi^{i_{\tau(2n-1)},i_{\tau(1)}}.

whereΨi,j\Psi^{i,j}denotes the Dodgson polynomial associated toLLandΨ\Psiis the graph polynomial (definition˜A.1).

## Proof.

By[15, Corollary 6.24]the canonical formβL2​n−1\beta_{L}^{2n-1}is equal totr⁡(ηL2​n−1)\operatorname{tr}(\eta_{L}^{2n-1})whereηL\eta_{L}is defined as the2​n×2​n2n\times 2n-matrix of11-forms given by(ηL)i​j=(Ψi​jΨ​d​xj),1≤i≤j≤2​n.(\eta_{L})_{ij}=\left(\frac{\Psi^{ij}}{\Psi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{j}\right),\quad 1\leq i\leq j\leq 2n.

Expanding the matrix product yields(10)βL2​n−1=tr⁡(ηL2​n−1)=1Ψ2​n−1​∑k1,…,k2​n−1=12​nΨk1,k2​Ψk2,k3​…​Ψk2​n−1,k1​d​xk2∧…∧d​xk2​n−1∧d​xk1.\beta_{L}^{2n-1}=\operatorname{tr}(\eta_{L}^{2n-1})=\frac{1}{\Psi^{2n-1}}\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{2n-1}=1}^{2n}\Psi^{k_{1},k_{2}}\Psi^{k_{2},k_{3}}\ldots\Psi^{k_{2n-1},k_{1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k_{2n-1}}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k_{1}}.

Sinced​xkl∧d​xkm=0\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k_{l}}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k_{m}}=0forl=ml=m, only terms for which the indicesk1,…,k2​n−1k_{1},\ldots,k_{2n-1}are pairwise distinct
contribute to the sum.
As there are exactly2​n−12n-1such indices, choosing values fork1,…,k2​n−1k_{1},\ldots,k_{2n-1}is equivalent to choosing one elementj∈[2​n]j\in[2n]to omit and then choosing a permutation of the remaining elementsIj=[2​n]∖{j}I_{j}=[2n]\setminus\{j\}.
Thus,eq.˜10becomesβL2​n−1=1Ψ2​n−1​∑j∈[2​n]∑τ∈𝕊2​n−1Ψiτ​(1),iτ​(2)​Ψiτ​(2),iτ​(3)​…​Ψiτ​(2​n−1),iτ​(1)​d​xiτ​(1)∧…∧d​xiτ​(2​n−1).\beta_{L}^{2n-1}=\frac{1}{\Psi^{2n-1}}\sum_{j\in[2n]}\sum_{\tau\in\mathbb{S}_{2n-1}}\Psi^{i_{\tau(1)},i_{\tau(2)}}\Psi^{i_{\tau(2)},i_{\tau(3)}}\ldots\Psi^{i_{\tau(2n-1)},i_{\tau(1)}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i_{\tau(1)}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i_{\tau(2n-1)}}.

Reordering the differential forms into increasing orderd​xi1∧…∧d​xi2​n−1\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i_{2n-1}}produces precisely
the signsgn⁡(τ)\operatorname{sgn}(\tau). Hence(11)βL2​n−1=∑j∈[2​n]fj​(x)​d​x1∧⋯∧d​xj^∧⋯∧d​x2​n.\beta_{L}^{2n-1}=\sum_{j\in[2n]}f_{j}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{j}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{2n}.

Finally,[15, Theorem 2.1]shows thatβL2​n−1\beta_{L}^{2n-1}is a projective differential form.
Therefore, its contraction with the Euler vector fieldE=∑i=12​nxi​∂∂xiE=\sum_{i=1}^{2n}x_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}vanishes,ιE​βL2​n−1=∑j,k∈[2​n]k<j((−1)j​xj​fk​(x)−(−1)k​xk​fj​(x))​⋀i≠j,kd​xi=0,\displaystyle\iota_{E}\beta_{L}^{2n-1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\in[2n]\\
k<j\end{subarray}}\left((-1)^{j}x_{j}f_{k}(x)-(-1)^{k}x_{k}f_{j}(x)\right)\bigwedge_{i\neq j,k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}=0,

and(−1)k​fk​(x)xk=(−1)j​fj​(x)xj(-1)^{k}\frac{f_{k}(x)}{x_{k}}=(-1)^{j}\frac{f_{j}(x)}{x_{j}}for allj,k∈[2​n]j,k\in[2n].
We conclude thateq.˜11is equal to(−1)d​fd​(x)xd​∑j=12​n(−1)j​xj​d​x1∧⋯∧d​xj^∧⋯∧d​x2​n=(−1)d+1​fd​(x)xd​Ω​(x).∎(-1)^{d}\frac{f_{d}(x)}{x_{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{2n}(-1)^{j}x_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{j}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{2n}=(-1)^{d+1}\frac{f_{d}(x)}{x_{d}}\Omega(x).\qed

Finally, we record three vanishing properties of the canonical forms:

## Proposition 3.12.

The canonical formβL2​n−1\beta^{2n-1}_{L}vanishes whenever one of the following conditions holds:
- a)

nnis even,
- b)

there existi∈[2​n]i\in[2n]such thatrank⁡(Ai,∙)<n\operatorname{rank}(A^{i,\bullet})<n,
- c)

AAhas a repeated row, i.e. there existi≠ji\neq jin[2​n][2n]such thatai=aja_{i}=a_{j}.

## Proof.

We first prove a). By Property33of[15, Lemma 4.3],βL2​n−1=(−1)n+1​βLT2​n−1\beta^{2n-1}_{L}=(-1)^{n+1}\beta_{L^{T}}^{2n-1}.
SinceLLis symmetric, the vanishing for evennnfollows trivially.

We now prove b).
As in the proof ofproposition˜3.7, after replacingAAbyA​PAPfor a suitable matrixP∈GLn⁡(ℝ)P\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}),
we may assume that the first column ofAi,∙A^{i,\bullet}vanishes identically.
ThenLLhas block formL=(x100R).L=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&0\\
0&R\\
\end{pmatrix}.

whereR∈ℝ​[x2,…,x2​n](n−1)×(n−1)R\in\mathbb{R}[x_{2},\ldots,x_{2n}]^{(n-1)\times(n-1)}is independent ofx1x_{1}. Consequently,βL2​n−1=tr⁡((L−1​d​L)2​n−1)\displaystyle\beta^{2n-1}_{L}=\operatorname{tr}((L^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}L)^{2n-1})=tr⁡((d​x1x100R−1​d​R)2​n−1)\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}(\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}&0\\
0&R^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}R\end{pmatrix}^{2n-1})=tr⁡(((d​x1x1)2​n−100(R−1​d​R)2​n−1))=(d​x1x1)2​n−1+βR2​n−1=0.\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}(\begin{pmatrix}(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}})^{2n-1}&0\\
0&(R^{-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}R)^{2n-1}\end{pmatrix})=\left(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}\right)^{2n-1}+\beta^{2n-1}_{R}=0.

where the first term vanished trivially sinced​xi∧d​xi=0\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}=0and the second term also
vanished becauseRRdepends on only2​n−12n-1variables, soβR2​n−1\beta_{R}^{2n-1}is a(2​n−1)(2n-1)-form on a space of dimension2​n−22n-2.

Finally, we prove c). By permuting the rows ofAA, we may assume thata1=a2a_{1}=a_{2},
sinceβL2​n−1\beta_{L}^{2n-1}is unaffected except for a relabelling of the coordinatesxix_{i}. ExpandingLLas a linear function of the coordinates
givesL=x1​(a1T​a1)+…+x2​n​(a2​nT​a2​n)=(x1+x2)​(a1T​a1)+x3​(a3T​a3)+…+x2​n​(a2​nT​a2​n).L=x_{1}(a_{1}^{T}a_{1})+\ldots+x_{2n}(a_{2n}^{T}a_{2n})=(x_{1}+x_{2})(a_{1}^{T}a_{1})+x_{3}(a_{3}^{T}a_{3})+\ldots+x_{2n}(a_{2n}^{T}a_{2n}).

LetA~\widetilde{A}be the matrix whose rows are(a1,a3,…,a2​n)(a_{1},a_{3},\ldots,a_{2n}), and letL~=A~T​diag⁡(x1,…,x2​n−1)​A~\widetilde{L}=\widetilde{A}^{T}\operatorname{diag}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n-1})\widetilde{A}.

Consider the mapπ:ℝℙ>02​n−1∖{[1:−1:0:…:0]}→ℝℙ>02​n−2\pi:\mathbb{RP}^{2n-1}_{>0}\setminus\{[1:-1:0:\ldots:0]\}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2n-2}_{>0}given by(x1,…,x2​n)→(x1+x2,x3,…,x2​n)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n})\to(x_{1}+x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{2n}).
ThenLLis the pullback ofL~\widetilde{L}underπ\pi, and henceβL2​n−1=π∗​βL~2​n−1.\beta^{2n-1}_{L}=\pi^{*}\beta^{2n-1}_{\widetilde{L}}.

SinceβL~2​n−1\beta^{2n-1}_{\widetilde{L}}is a(2​n−1)(2n-1)-form on the(2​n−2)(2n-2)-dimensional spaceℝ​ℙ>02​n−2\mathbb{RP}^{2n-2}_{>0},
it must vanish identically. Therefore,βL2​n−1=0\beta_{L}^{2n-1}=0.
∎

With these properties of the RW- and canonical integrals established, we can now proceed to prove the main theorems.

## 4.Proofs of main theorems

We turn to the proof of the main theoremtheorem˜2.2, in full rigor.
Referring back tofigure˜3, the proof is divided as follows. Insection˜5, we establish the left-hand side of the diagram,
namely the passage from the auxiliary integral to the RW-integral.
Insection˜6, we prove the top-arrow on the right-hand side offigure˜3, corresponding to the complex integration.
Finally, insection˜7, we prove the final matrix identity needed to match the intermediate result with the canonical form.

In the proof given in this section, we combine these results and introduce the necessary regularising terms to ensure that all integrals
remain convergent.
Furthermore, we proveproposition˜1.13and show that the auxiliary integral is absolutely convergent, allowing us to integrate in either order.

## Theorem 4.1.

Letn≥3n\geq 3and letA∈ℝ2​n×nA\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times n}of ranknn, such that no row ofAAis equal to0. ThenIRW​(A)=Ican​(A).I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=I_{\operatorname{can}}(A).

Bypropositions˜3.7and3.12we may restrict to the case wherennis odd andrank⁡(Ai,∙)=n\operatorname{rank}(A_{i,\bullet})=nfor alli∈[2​n]i\in[2n].

Letε>0\varepsilon>0and define the truncated affine simplicesσ2​nε:={α∈ℝ≥ε2​n∣∑i=12​nαi=1}andσ~2​nε:={x∈ℝ≥02​n∣xi≤1ε​∀i∈[2​n]​and​∑i=12​n1xi=1}.\sigma_{2n}^{\varepsilon}:=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}_{\geq\varepsilon}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\alpha_{i}=1\}\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}\widetilde{\sigma}_{2n}^{\varepsilon}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2n}\mid x_{i}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\;\forall i\in[2n]\text{ and }\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\frac{1}{x_{i}}=1\}.

Then the regularised auxiliary integral is defined asIauxε​(A)=Γ​(2​n)(2​π​i)n​∑l∈[2​n]l≠d(−1)l+d+(d<l)\displaystyle I_{\operatorname{aux}}^{\varepsilon}(A)=\frac{\Gamma(2n)}{(2\pi i)^{n}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in[2n]\\
l\neq d\end{subarray}}(-1)^{l+d+(d<l)}(∫ℂn×σ2​nε⋀e≠l,d(𝒂e¯​d​𝒂e+𝒂e​d​𝒂e¯)∧d​𝒂d∧d​𝒂d¯(∑e≠lαe​|𝒂e|2+αl​|𝒂l|2+αl)2​nΩ(α)\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{e}+\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}})\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{d}}}{(\sum_{e\neq l}\alpha_{e}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l})^{2n}}\Omega(\alpha)\right.(12)−\displaystyle-∫ℂn×σ2​nε⋀e≠l,d(𝒂e¯​d​𝒂e+𝒂e​d​𝒂e¯)∧d​𝒂d∧d​𝒂d¯(∑e≠lαe​|𝒂e|2+αl​|𝒂d|2+αl)2​nΩ(α))\displaystyle\left.\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{e}+\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}})\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{d}}}{(\sum_{e\neq l}\alpha_{e}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l})^{2n}}\Omega(\alpha)\right)

Observe that the two integrals differ only in which linear form|𝒂i|2\left\lvert\bm{a}_{i}\right\rvert^{2}appears as the coefficient ofαl\alpha_{l}.
In the first integral this is|𝒂l|2\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}, while in the second it is|𝒂d|2\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}.
Translating this into the language of the matrixAA, the first term arises fromAA,
whereas the second arises from the matrix obtained fromAAby replacing thell-th row with thedd-th row ofAA.
To distinguish these cases clearly in the remainder of the proof, form∈[2​n]m\in[2n]we denote byAmA_{m}the matrix obtained fromAAby replacing itsll-th row with themm-th row ofAA.
Form=lm=l, this is simply the original matrixAA.
Thus, the first integral ineq.˜12is associated withAlA_{l}, and the second withAdA_{d}.

All expressions normally derived fromAAcan likewise be defined in terms ofAmA_{m}. We denote the Laplacian matrix
ofAmA_{m}byLmL_{m}, the graph matrix ofAmA_{m}byMmM_{m}, the First Symanzik polynomial ofAmA_{m}byΨm\Psi_{m}and, fori,j∈[2​n]i,j\in[2n], the corresponding Dodgson polynomial associated toAmA_{m}byΨmi,j\Psi^{i,j}_{m}.

first overσ2​nε\sigma_{2n}^{\varepsilon}and then overℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}. l\neq d\end{subarray}}(-1)^{l-1}\int_{C_{A}}\log\bigg(\frac{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}}\bigg)\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e}).

Adding it in we findlimε→0Iauxε​(A)\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}I_{\operatorname{aux}}^{\varepsilon}(A)=(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫CA∑l∈[2​n](−1)l−1​log⁡(|𝒂l|2|𝒂d|2)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)\displaystyle=\frac{(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{C_{A}}\sum_{l\in[2n]}(-1)^{l-1}\log\bigg(\frac{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}}\bigg)\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫CA∑l∈[2​n](−1)l−1​log⁡(|𝒂l|2)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)=IRW​(A).\displaystyle=\frac{(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{C_{A}}\sum_{l\in[2n]}(-1)^{l-1}\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2})\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A).

where in the second equality, we used the observation fromlemma˜3.2, that the following vanisheslog⁡(|𝒂d|2)​∑l=12​n(−1)l−1​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)=0.\log(\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2})\sum_{l=1}^{2n}(-1)^{l-1}\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=0.

This concludes one half of the proof.

To show that, in the limitε→0\varepsilon\to 0,Iauxε​(A)I_{\operatorname{aux}}^{\varepsilon}(A)is equal toIcan​(A)I_{\operatorname{can}}(A)we proceed as follows.
Letℐdl:={(S,T)⊆([2​n]∖{l,d})2∣|S|=|T|=n−1​and​S∩T=∅}\mathcal{I}_{d}^{l}:=\{(S,T)\subseteq([2n]\setminus\{l,d\})^{2}\mid\left\lvert S\right\rvert=\left\lvert T\right\rvert=n-1\text{ and }S\cap T=\varnothing\}denote the set of ordered bipartitions of[2​n]∖{l,d}[2n]\setminus\{l,d\}into two sets of sizen−1n-1.

l\neq d\end{subarray}}(∫σ~2​nε(−1)n+12+d+l+(l<d)Ψln∑S,T∈ℐdlsgn(S,T)det(AS​d,∙)det(AT​d,∙)perm(adj(Ml)S,T)Ω​(x)xd\displaystyle\left(\int_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{2n}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}+d+l+(l<d)}}{\Psi^{n}_{l}}\sum_{S,T\in\mathcal{I}_{d}^{l}}\operatorname{sgn}(S,T)\det(A_{Sd,\bullet})\det(A_{Td,\bullet})\operatorname{perm}(\operatorname{adj}(M_{l})_{S,T})\frac{\Omega(x)}{x_{d}}\right.−\displaystyle-∫σ~2​nε(−1)n+12+d+l+(l<d)Ψdn∑S,T∈ℐdlsgn(S,T)det(AS​d,∙)det(AT​d,∙)perm(adj(Md)S,T)Ω​(x)xd).\displaystyle\left.\int_{\widetilde{\sigma}_{2n}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}+d+l+(l<d)}}{\Psi^{n}_{d}}\sum_{S,T\in\mathcal{I}_{d}^{l}}\operatorname{sgn}(S,T)\det(A_{Sd,\bullet})\det(A_{Td,\bullet})\operatorname{perm}(\operatorname{adj}(M_{d})_{S,T})\frac{\Omega(x)}{x_{d}}\right).

Both integrals are absolutely convergent for allε>0\varepsilon>0. Forε=0\varepsilon=0, the domain of integration becomesσ~2​n0={x∈ℝ≥02​n∣∑i=12​n1xi=1}\widetilde{\sigma}_{2n}^{0}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}_{\geq 0}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\frac{1}{x_{i}}=1\right\},
which is just a parametrisation of the projective simplexσ2​n\sigma_{2n}from the introduction.

Therefore, forε=0\varepsilon=0the integrals coincide with the canonical integrals forAlA_{l}andAdA_{d}, and are thus absolutely convergent.

Finally, thatAdA_{d}contains thedd-th row twice: once as rowddand once as
rowll. Hence, byproposition˜3.12, the second integral vanishes.

that is that the sum over the choice of logarithm edge in the definition ofIRWI_{\operatorname{RW}}can be removed:

## Proposition 4.2.

Letn≥3n\geq 3, and letA∈ℝ2​n×nA\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times n}of ranknn, such that no row ofAAis equal to0.
Further, letl,d∈[2​n]l,d\in[2n]withl≠dl\neq d, then(13)IR​W​(A)=(2​n−1)​(−1)l−1​(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫CAlog⁡(|𝒂l|2|𝒂d|2)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e).I_{RW}(A)=(2n-1)\frac{(-1)^{l-1}(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{C_{A}}\log\bigg(\frac{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}}\bigg)\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e}).

Moreover, on the reduced spaceZA=ConfA⁡(ℂ)/ℂ∗Z_{A}=\operatorname{Conf}_{A}(\mathbb{C})/\mathbb{C}^{*}, this givesIR​W​(A)=(2​n−1)​(−1)l+d+(l<d)(2​π​i)n−1​∫ZAlog⁡(|𝒂l|2|𝒂d|2)​⋀e≠l,ddlog⁡(|𝒂|e2).I_{RW}(A)=(2n-1)\frac{(-1)^{l+d+(l<d)}}{(2\pi i)^{n-1}}\int_{Z_{A}}\log\bigg(\frac{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}}\bigg)\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\operatorname{dlog}(\left\lvert\bm{a}\right\rvert_{e}^{2}).

## Proof.

Letl,d∈[2​n]l,d\in[2n]withl≠dl\neq d.
Observe, that in the formula for the canonical form inproposition˜3.11the product of
Dodgson polynomials is invariant under the cyclic permutation(12​…​2​n−1)(12\ldots 2n-1). where(j1,…,j2​n−2)=[2​n]∖{l,d}(j_{1},\ldots,j_{2n-2})=[2n]\setminus\{l,d\}.
Tracing back through the proof oftheorem˜7.1this is exactly equal to just one term for a fixedllin the sum overll, that isβL2​n−1=(2n−1)(−1)n+12+d+l+(l<d)xd​Ψn∑S,T∈ℐdlsgn(S,T)det(AS​d,∙)det(AT​d,∙)perm(adj(M)S,T)Ω(x)\beta^{2n-1}_{L}=(2n-1)\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}+d+l+(l<d)}}{x_{d}\Psi^{n}}\sum_{S,T\in\mathcal{I}_{d}^{l}}\operatorname{sgn}(S,T)\det(A_{Sd,\bullet})\det(A_{Td,\bullet})\operatorname{perm}(\operatorname{adj}(M)_{S,T})\Omega(x)

which are given for fixedll, to the canonical integral
ofβL2​n−1\beta^{2n-1}_{L}as given above, to obtainIRW​(A)=Ican​(A)=∫σ2​nβL2​n−1=(−1)l−1​(2​i)2​n−12​(2​π​i)n​∫CAlog⁡(|𝒂l|2|𝒂d|2)​⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e).I_{\operatorname{RW}}(A)=I_{\operatorname{can}}(A)=\int_{\sigma_{2n}}\beta^{2n-1}_{L}=\frac{(-1)^{l-1}(2i)^{2n-1}}{2(2\pi i)^{n}}\int_{C_{A}}\log\bigg(\frac{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}}\bigg)\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e}).

which proves the first statement.

## 4.1.Absolute convergence of the auxiliary integral

## Theorem 4.3.

Letn≥3n\geq 3odd, letε>0\varepsilon>0, letl,d,m∈[2​n]l,d,m\in[2n]withl≠dl\neq dandA∈ℝ2​n×nA\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times n}such thatrank⁡(Ai,∙)=n\operatorname{rank}(A_{i,\bullet})=nfor alli∈[2​n]i\in[2n].
Then the following integral converges absolutely(14)∫ℂn×σ2​nε⋀e≠l,d(𝒂e¯​d​𝒂e+𝒂e​d​𝒂e¯)∧d​𝒂d∧d​𝒂d¯(∑e≠lαe​|𝒂e|2+αl​|𝒂m|2+αl)2​n​Ω​(α).\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{e}+\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}})\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{d}}}{(\sum_{e\neq l}\alpha_{e}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{m}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l})^{2n}}\Omega(\alpha).

## Proof.

We prove the absolute convergence by compactifying the integration domain. Letπ:ℂℙn∖{z0=0}→ℂn,[z0:z1:…:zn]↦(z1z0,…,znz0)\pi:\mathbb{CP}^{n}\setminus\{z_{0}=0\}\to\mathbb{C}^{n},[z_{0}:z_{1}:\ldots:z_{n}]\mapsto\left(\frac{z_{1}}{z_{0}},\ldots,\frac{z_{n}}{z_{0}}\right)be the standard affine chart.
Pulling back each term of the wedge product in the numerator ineq.˜14byπ\pigivesπ∗​(𝒂e¯​d​𝒂e+𝒂e​d​𝒂e¯)=𝒂e¯​d​𝒂e+𝒂e​d​𝒂e¯|z0|2−|𝒂e|2|z0|2​(d​z0z0+d​z0¯z0¯)andπ∗​(d​𝒂d)=d​𝒂dz0−𝒂d​d​z0z02.\pi^{*}\left(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{e}+\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\right)=\frac{\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{e}+\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}}{|z_{0}|^{2}}-\frac{|\bm{a}_{e}|^{2}}{|z_{0}|^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{0}}{z_{0}}+\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{z_{0}}}{\overline{z_{0}}}\right)\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}\pi^{*}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d})=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d}}{z_{0}}-\bm{a}_{d}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{0}}{z_{0}^{2}}.

Importantly,d​z0\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{0}andd​z0¯\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{z_{0}}can appear at most once and therefore the full numerator pulls back to an expression of the formπ∗​(⋀e≠l,d(𝒂e¯​d​ae+𝒂e​d​𝒂e¯)∧d​𝒂d∧d​𝒂d¯)=Q​(z,z¯)|z0|4​n​(∑i,j=0nzi​zj¯​(d​zj∧d​zi¯)​⋀e≠i,jd​ze∧d​ze¯)\pi^{*}\bigg(\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}a_{e}+\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}})\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{d}}\bigg)=\frac{Q(z,\overline{z})}{|z_{0}|^{4n}}\left(\sum_{i,j=0}^{n}z_{i}\overline{z_{j}}\left(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{j}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{z_{i}}\right)\bigwedge_{e\neq i,j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z_{e}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{z_{e}}\right)

Here,Q​(z,z¯)Q(z,\overline{z})is a polynomial in the homogeneous coordinates and the expression in parentheses
is the standard volume form onℂ​ℙn\mathbb{CP}^{n}which we denote byΩ​([z])\Omega([z]).
Pulling back the denominator ineq.˜14byπ\piwe obtainπ∗​(∑e≠lαe​|𝒂e|2+αl​|𝒂m|2+αl)2​n=(∑e≠lαe​|𝒂e|2+αl​|𝒂m|2+αl)2​n|z0|4​n.\pi^{*}\bigg(\sum_{e\neq l}\alpha_{e}|\bm{a}_{e}|^{2}+\alpha_{l}|\bm{a}_{m}|^{2}+\alpha_{l}\bigg)^{2n}=\frac{(\sum_{e\neq l}\alpha_{e}|\bm{a}_{e}|^{2}+\alpha_{l}|\bm{a}_{m}|^{2}+\alpha_{l})^{2n}}{|z_{0}|^{4n}}.

Hence, thez0z_{0}-factor from the numerator exactly cancels thez0z_{0}-factor coming from the denominator and the whole integrand becomes∫ℂ​ℙn×σ2​nε[Q​(z,z¯)(∑e≠lαe​|𝒂e|2+αl​|𝒂m|2+αl​|z0|2)2​n]​Ω​([z])​Ω​(α)\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}^{\varepsilon}}\left[\frac{Q(z,\overline{z})}{(\sum_{e\neq l}\alpha_{e}|\bm{a}_{e}|^{2}+\alpha_{l}|\bm{a}_{m}|^{2}+\alpha_{l}|z_{0}|^{2})^{2n}}\right]\Omega([z])\Omega(\alpha)

Letz=(z1,…,zn)z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}). We denote the function in brackets byg([z0:z],α)g([z_{0}:z],\alpha).
It remains to show thatg([z0:z],α)g([z_{0}:z],\alpha)is continuous onℂ​ℙn×σ2​nε\mathbb{CP}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}^{\varepsilon}.
Sinceαi≥ε\alpha_{i}\geq\varepsilonfor alli∈[2​n]i\in[2n], and the denominator is a sum of non-negative terms, it can vanish only if𝒂e​(z)=0​for all​e≠l,𝒂m​(z)=0andz0=0.\bm{a}_{e}(z)=0\text{ for all }e\neq l,\quad\bm{a}_{m}(z)=0\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}z_{0}=0.

By assumptionrank⁡(Al,∙)=n\operatorname{rank}(A_{l,\bullet})=n, so the linear forms(𝒂e)e≠l(\bm{a}_{e})_{e\neq l}span(ℂn)∗(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{*}.
Hence𝒂e​(z)=0\bm{a}_{e}(z)=0for alle≠le\neq limpliesz=(z1,…,zn)=(0,…,0)z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=(0,\ldots,0).
Together withz0=0z_{0}=0, this would imply that all homogeneous coordinatesziz_{i}(0≤i≤n0\leq i\leq n) vanish.
This however is impossible as the origin in not a point inℂ​ℙn\mathbb{CP}^{n}.
Therefore, the denominator is strictly positive onℂ​ℙn×σ2​nε\mathbb{CP}^{n}\times\sigma_{2n}^{\varepsilon}andggis continuous.

Consequentlyggis bounded asℂ​ℙn\mathbb{CP}^{n}andσ2​nε\sigma_{2n}^{\varepsilon}are compact. As the spaces also have finite measure,
the integral of|g|\left\lvert g\right\rvertover their product is finite.
This proves the absolute convergence.
∎

## 5.From product space to position space - Theorem5.1

We show the first step in provingtheorem˜2.2by showing that the auxiliary integral is equal to

## Theorem 5.1.

Letn≥3n\geq 3odd,ε>0\varepsilon>0,l,d∈[2​n]l,d\in[2n]such thatl≠dl\neq dandA∈ℝ2​n×nA\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times n}such thatrank⁡(Ai,∙)=n\operatorname{rank}(A_{i,\bullet})=nfor alli∈[2​n]i\in[2n]and no row ofAAis equal to0.

\left(\frac{\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{e}+\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}})\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{d}}}{(\sum_{e\neq l}\alpha_{e}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l})^{n}}-\frac{\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}(\overline{\bm{a}_{e}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{e}+\bm{a}_{e}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{e}})\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{d}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{\bm{a}_{d}}}{(\sum_{e\neq l}\alpha_{e}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l}\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}+\alpha_{l})^{n}}\right)\Omega(\alpha).

The idea of the proof is to use the Feynman parametrisation to rewrite the integrand.
However, for this to work properly, we have to regularise it:

## 5.1.Regularised Feynman parametrisation

The classic Feynman parametrisationproposition˜2.5is only defined when allAiA_{i}are nonzero.
For our purpose, we need to extend this to allow someAiA_{i}to go to zero.
For simplicity we reduce to the case where all exponentss1,…,sns_{1},\ldots,s_{n}are equal to11.
We find the following regularised identity:

## Theorem 5.2(Regularised Feynman parametrisation).

Letn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, letA1,…,An∈ℝ≥0A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}not all zero and defineA:=∑i=1nAiA:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}.

## Proof.

Since at least oneAiA_{i}is nonzero,A>0A>0and the classic Feynman parametrisation applies1t​∏i=1n1(Ai+εt​A)=Γ​(n)t​∫σn01(∑i=1nβi​Ai+εt​A)n​Ω​(β),\frac{1}{t}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(A_{i}+\frac{\varepsilon}{t}A)}=\frac{\Gamma(n)}{t}\int_{\sigma^{0}_{n}}\frac{1}{(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}A_{i}+\frac{\varepsilon}{t}A)^{n}}\Omega(\beta),

where we used that∑i=1nβi=1\sum_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}=1.
Consider the mapφ:σnε→σn0,α↦α−εt\varphi:\sigma_{n}^{\varepsilon}\to\sigma_{n}^{0},\alpha\mapsto\frac{\alpha-\varepsilon}{t}.
One computesφ​(σn0)=σnε\varphi(\sigma^{0}_{n})=\sigma_{n}^{\varepsilon}and usingφ\varphias a change of variables finds(15)1t​∏i=1n1(Ai+εt​A)=Γ​(n)​∫σnεtn−1(∑i=1nαi​Ai)n​Ω​(α−εt).\frac{1}{t}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(A_{i}+\frac{\varepsilon}{t}A)}=\Gamma(n)\int_{\sigma_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\frac{t^{n-1}}{(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}A_{i})^{n}}\Omega\left(\frac{\alpha-\varepsilon}{t}\right).

On the simplexσn\sigma_{n}, consider the affine chart given byαn=1−∑i=1n−1αi\alpha_{n}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\alpha_{i}. In these coordinates,Ω​(α)=(−1)n−1​d​α1​…​d​αn−1andS​(α):=∑i=1n(−1)n​⋀e≠id​αe=n​(−1)n−1​d​α1​…​d​αn−1.\Omega(\alpha)=(-1)^{n-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}\ldots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha_{n-1}\mathrel{\quad\text{and}\quad}S(\alpha):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{n}\bigwedge_{e\neq i}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha_{e}=n(-1)^{n-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}\ldots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha_{n-1}.

A direct computation yieldsΩ​(α−εt)=1tn​Ω​(α)−εtn​S​(α)=1tn​(1−n​ε)​(−1)n−1​d​α1​…​d​αn−1=Ω​(α)tn−1.\Omega\left(\frac{\alpha-\varepsilon}{t}\right)=\frac{1}{t^{n}}\Omega(\alpha)-\frac{\varepsilon}{t^{n}}S(\alpha)=\frac{1}{t^{n}}(1-n\varepsilon)(-1)^{n-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}\ldots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha_{n-1}=\frac{\Omega(\alpha)}{t^{n-1}}.

Substituting intoeq.˜15completes the proof.

## Lemma 5.3.

Letn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, letAi∈ℝ>0A_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}andbi∈ℝ>0b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}fori∈[n]i\in[n]and letm∈ℕm\in\mathbb{N},m<n−1m<n-1, thenrm∏i=1n(Ai​r+bi)=(−1)m​∑i=1nciAi​r+biwherecj=(−bj)m​Ajn−1−m∏k≠j(Aj​bk−Ak​bj).\frac{r^{m}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(A_{i}r+b_{i})}=(-1)^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{c_{i}}{A_{i}r+b_{i}}\mathrel{\quad\text{where}\quad}c_{j}=\frac{(-b_{j})^{m}A_{j}^{n-1-m}}{\prod_{k\neq j}(A_{j}b_{k}-A_{k}b_{j})}.

Further, thecic_{i}satisfy∑i=1nciAi=0\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{c_{i}}{A_{i}}=0.

## Proof.

This is mostly an exercise in partial fraction decomposition. That is, we want to findci∈ℝc_{i}\in\mathbb{R}such that(16)rm∏i=1n(Ai​r+bi)=∑i=1nciAi​r+bi⇒rm=∑i=1nci​∏k≠i(Ak​r+bk),\frac{r^{m}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(A_{i}r+b_{i})}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{c_{i}}{A_{i}r+b_{i}}\Rightarrow r^{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\prod_{k\neq i}(A_{k}r+b_{k}),

where we multiplied both sides by∏i=1n(Ai​r+bi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}(A_{i}r+b_{i}).
Evaluating atr=−bjAjr=-\frac{b_{j}}{A_{j}}forj∈[n]j\in[n]gives(−bjAj)m=∑i=1nci​∏k≠i(Ak​(−bjAj)+bk)=cjAjn−1​∏k≠j(Aj​bk−Ak​bj),\left(-\frac{b_{j}}{A_{j}}\right)^{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\prod_{k\neq i}\left(A_{k}\left(-\frac{b_{j}}{A_{j}}\right)+b_{k}\right)=\frac{c_{j}}{A_{j}^{n-1}}\prod_{k\neq j}(A_{j}b_{k}-A_{k}b_{j})\\
,

and solving forcjc_{j}proves the first part.
Further, takeeq.˜16and compare coefficients ofrn−1r^{n-1}on both sides. Asm<n−1m<n-1this gives0=(∏i=1nAi)​∑i=1nciAi0=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{c_{i}}{A_{i}}and thus∑i=1nciAi=0\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{c_{i}}{A_{i}}=0, which concludes the proof.

appear.

In the next step we integrate over the scale. For this, consider the mapφ:CA×ℝ≥0→V,(z,r)↦r​z\varphi:C_{A}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to V,(z,r)\mapsto\sqrt{r}zwhereV={z∈ℂn∣𝒂i​(z)≠0​∀i∈[2​n]}V=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mid\bm{a}_{i}(z)\neq 0\;\forall i\in[2n]\}.

## Proposition 5.5.

For anyγ<1\gamma<1the integrand ineq.˜18is bounded absolutely byg:{z∈ℂn∣|z|=1​and​𝐚i​(z)≠0​∀i∈[2​n]}→ℂg:\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\mid\left\lvert z\right\rvert=1\text{ and }\bm{a}_{i}(z)\neq 0\;\forall i\in[2n]\}\to\mathbb{C}given byg​(z):=C​|1+log⁡(|𝒂l|2|𝒂d|2)|​|⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)|.g(z):=C\left\lvert 1+\log\bigg(\frac{|\bm{a}_{l}|^{2}}{|\bm{a}_{d}|^{2}}\bigg)\right\rvert\left|\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})\right|.

forC∈ℝ>0C\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}. The functionggis absolutely integrable on its domain.

Before proving the proposition we finish the proof oftheorem˜5.1.
Asγ=ε1−2​n​ε\gamma=\frac{\varepsilon}{1-2n\varepsilon}is invertible we can change the limit fromε→0\varepsilon\to 0toγ→0\gamma\to 0.
Under this transformationt=11+n​γt=\frac{1}{1+n\gamma}.
Byproposition˜5.5the dominated convergence theorem (theorem˜B.2)
applies to the integral ineq.˜18and we can take the limit inγ\gammaon the integrand ofeq.˜18to find that the RHS oftheorem˜5.1is equal to(2​i)2​n−12​∫CAlog⁡(|𝒂l|2|𝒂d|2)​⋀e≠l,ddarg⁡(𝒂e),\frac{(2i)^{2n-1}}{2}\int_{C_{A}}\log\bigg(\frac{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{l}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lvert\bm{a}_{d}\right\rvert^{2}}\bigg)\bigwedge_{e\neq l,d}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e}),

which concludes the proof oftheorem˜5.1. ∎

To proveproposition˜5.5we need the following additional lemma:

## Lemma 5.6.

Letw∈ℝ≥0w\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}andu,v∈ℝ>0u,v\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}then|log⁡(u+wv+w)|≤|log⁡(uv)|.\left\lvert\log\left(\frac{u+w}{v+w}\right)\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\log\left(\frac{u}{v}\right)\right\rvert.

## Proof.

Observe first that1≤u+wv+w≤uvifu>vanduv≤u+wv+w≤1if​u<v1\leq\frac{u+w}{v+w}\leq\frac{u}{v}\mathrel{\quad\text{ if $u>v$ and }\quad}\frac{u}{v}\leq\frac{u+w}{v+w}\leq 1\qquad\text{if }u<v

Hence, it follows that|log⁡(u+wv+w)|={log⁡(u+wv+w)if​u>v−log⁡(u+wv+w)if​u<v≤{log⁡(uv)if​u>v−log⁡(uv)if​u<v=|log⁡(uv)|\left\lvert\log\left(\frac{u+w}{v+w}\right)\right\rvert=\begin{cases}\log(\frac{u+w}{v+w})&\text{ if }u>v\\
-\log(\frac{u+w}{v+w})&\text{ if }u<v\\
\end{cases}\leq\begin{cases}\log(\frac{u}{v})&\text{ if }u>v\\
-\log(\frac{u}{v})&\text{ if }u<v\\
\end{cases}=\left\lvert\log\left(\frac{u}{v}\right)\right\rvert

aslog⁡(x)\log(x)is monotonically increasing and−log⁡(x)-\log(x)is monotonically decreasing.
∎

To prove the absolute convergence of the integral ofggoverYAY_{A}we first compactifyYAY_{A}.
This is done by iteratively performing real-oriented blow-ups along the strataHI:=⋂i∈I{𝒂i​(z)=0}H_{I}:=\bigcap_{i\in I}\{\bm{a}_{i}(z)=0\},
starting with the highest codimension strata and proceeding in decreasing codimension.
Locally, this replaces the normal directions to each stratum by their sphere of directions, thereby recording the
relative rates and arguments with which the𝒂i\bm{a}_{i}tend to zero.
This gives a compact manifold with cornersYA¯\overline{Y_{A}}, following the Axelrod–Singer construction[1].
OnYA¯\overline{Y_{A}}, each angular map𝒂e/|𝒂e|\bm{a}_{e}/\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvertextends continuously to the boundary, so the formsdarg⁡(𝒂e)\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})extend with controlled boundary behaviour. Thus we can write⋀e≠ldarg⁡(𝒂e)=h​(z)​volYA¯.\bigwedge_{e\neq l}\operatorname{darg}(\bm{a}_{e})=h(z)\operatorname{vol}_{\overline{Y_{A}}}.

wherehhis continuous onYA¯\overline{Y_{A}}andvolYA¯\operatorname{vol}_{\overline{Y_{A}}}is a smooth volume form.
SinceYA¯\overline{Y_{A}}is compact,|h​(z)|\left\lvert h(z)\right\rvertis bounded by a constantM∈ℝ>0M\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}. Hence∫YAg​(z)≤C​M​∫YA¯(1+|log⁡(|𝒂l|2)|+|log⁡(|𝒂d|2)|)​volYA¯.\int_{Y_{A}}g(z)\leq CM\int_{\overline{Y_{A}}}\left(1+\left|\log(|\bm{a}_{l}|^{2})\right|+\left|\log(|\bm{a}_{d}|^{2})\right|\right)\operatorname{vol}_{\overline{Y_{A}}}.

It remains only to note that the logarithmic singularities are integrable..
In local coordinates near the boundary, we can choose variablesri∈(0,ci]r_{i}\in(0,c_{i}]that measure the distance to the boundary,
so that|𝒂e|\left\lvert\bm{a}_{e}\right\rvertis, up to multiplication by a bounded non-zero function, of the formr1​⋯​rkr_{1}\cdots r_{k}.
Thus the logarithmic terms are locally bounded by finite sums of the form∑i=1k|log⁡(ri)|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert\log(r_{i})\right\rvertnearri=0r_{i}=0,
and it suffices to check that such functions are integrable, which implies the absolute integrability ofgg,∫0cilog⁡(ri)​d​ri=ci​(log⁡(ci)−1)<∞.∎\int_{0}^{c_{i}}\log(r_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r_{i}=c_{i}(\log(c_{i})-1)<\infty.\qed

## Lemma 6.5.

LetPPbe a differential operator in{p,p¯}\{p,\overline{p}\}of degree(k,l)(k,l)(kkholomorphic andllanti-holomorphic derivatives).
Let|pi|2<ε28​n\left\lvert p_{i}\right\rvert^{2}<\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8n}, then the following bound holds:|P​1(zT​Lm​z¯+pT​Am​z+p¯T​Am​z¯+αl)s|≤g​(z):=(2ε)s+k+l​Γ​(s+k+l)​|F​(z,z¯)|(|Am​z|2+1)s+k+l,\left\lvert P\frac{1}{(z^{T}L_{m}\overline{z}+p^{T}A_{m}z+\overline{p}^{T}A_{m}\overline{z}+\alpha_{l})^{s}}\right\rvert\leq g(z):=\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)^{s+k+l}\frac{\Gamma(s+k+l)\left\lvert F(z,\overline{z})\right\rvert}{(|A_{m}z|^{2}+1)^{s+k+l}},

whereFFis some homogeneous polynomial in{z,z¯}\{z,\overline{z}\}of degree(k,l)(k,l).

## Proof.

Apply the differential operator to the fraction. To bound the denominator independently ofppandp¯\overline{p}we complete the square for each line ofAmA_{m}separately.
For this denotey=Am​zy=A_{m}zandβ=(α1,…,αl−1,αm,αl+1,…,α2​n)T\beta=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l-1},\alpha_{m},\alpha_{l+1},\ldots,\alpha_{2n})^{T}:zT​AmT​Dm​Am​z¯+pT​Am​z+p¯T​Am​z¯+αl\displaystyle z^{T}A_{m}^{T}D_{m}A_{m}\overline{z}+p^{T}A_{m}z+\overline{p}^{T}A_{m}\overline{z}+\alpha_{l}=αl+∑i=12​nβi​yi​yi¯+pi​yi+pi¯​yi¯\displaystyle=\alpha_{l}+\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\beta_{i}y_{i}\overline{y_{i}}+p_{i}y_{i}+\overline{p_{i}}\overline{y_{i}}=αl+∑i=12​nβi2​|yi+2​pi¯βi|2+∑i=12​nβi2​|yi|2−∑i=12​n2​|pi|2βi\displaystyle=\alpha_{l}+\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\frac{\beta_{i}}{2}\left\lvert y_{i}+\frac{2\overline{p_{i}}}{\beta_{i}}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\frac{\beta_{i}}{2}\left\lvert y_{i}\right\rvert^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\frac{2\left\lvert p_{i}\right\rvert^{2}}{\beta_{i}}≥ε+∑i=12​nβi2​|yi|2−2​∑i=12​n|pi|2βi\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon+\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\frac{\beta_{i}}{2}\left\lvert y_{i}\right\rvert^{2}-2\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\frac{\left\lvert p_{i}\right\rvert^{2}}{\beta_{i}}≥ε2|y|2+ε−4nε28​n​ε=ε2(|Amz|2)+1),\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|y|^{2}+\varepsilon-4n\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8n\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}(|A_{m}z|^{2})+1),

where we used thatαl>ε\alpha_{l}>\varepsilon,βi>ε\beta_{i}>\varepsilonand|pi|2<ε216​n\left\lvert p_{i}\right\rvert^{2}<\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{16n}for alli∈[2​n]i\in[2n].
Thus we boundeq.˜20asΓ​(s+k+l)​|F​(z,z¯)||zT​Lm​z¯+pT​Am​z+p¯T​Am​z¯+αl|s+k+l≤g​(z):=(2ε)s+k+l​Γ​(s+k+l)​|F​(z,z¯)|(|Am​z|2+1)s+k+l.\frac{\Gamma(s+k+l)\left\lvert F(z,\overline{z})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert z^{T}L_{m}\overline{z}+p^{T}A_{m}z+\overline{p}^{T}A_{m}\overline{z}+\alpha_{l}\right\rvert^{s+k+l}}\leq g(z):=\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)^{s+k+l}\frac{\Gamma(s+k+l)\left\lvert F(z,\overline{z})\right\rvert}{(|A_{m}z|^{2}+1)^{s+k+l}}.

This finishes the first part. What remains is to show thatggis absolutely integrable onℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}.

To this end, consider the integral ofggoverℂn\mathbb{C}^{n}and pull it back to complex projective space via[y0:y]↦yy0[y_{0}:y]\mapsto\frac{y}{y_{0}}:∫ℂn(2ε)s+k+l​Γ​(s+k+l)​|F​(z,z¯)|(|Am​z|2+1)s+k+l​Ω​(z)=∫ℂ​ℙn(2ε)s+k+l​Γ​(s+k+l)​|F​(y,y¯)|(|Am​y|2+|y0|2)s+k+l​|y0|2​s+2​k+2​ly0k​y0¯l​|y0|2​n+2​(∑i,j=0nyi​yj¯​(d​yj∧d​yi¯)​⋀e≠i,jd​ye∧d​ye¯).\int_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)^{s+k+l}\frac{\Gamma(s+k+l)\left\lvert F(z,\overline{z})\right\rvert}{(|A_{m}z|^{2}+1)^{s+k+l}}\Omega(z)\\
=\int_{\mathbb{CP}^{n}}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\right)^{s+k+l}\frac{\Gamma(s+k+l)\left\lvert F(y,\overline{y})\right\rvert}{(|A_{m}y|^{2}+|y_{0}|^{2})^{s+k+l}}\frac{|y_{0}|^{2s+2k+2l}}{y_{0}^{k}\overline{y_{0}}^{l}|y_{0}|^{2n+2}}\left(\sum_{i,j=0}^{n}y_{i}\overline{y_{j}}\left(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y_{j}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{y_{i}}\right)\bigwedge_{e\neq i,j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y_{e}\wedge\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\overline{y_{e}}\right).

The expression in parentheses is the standard volume form onℂ​ℙn\mathbb{CP}^{n}.
The crucial observation is that the integrand is smooth. Indeed,|Am​y|22+|y0|2≠0|A_{m}y|_{2}^{2}+\left\lvert y_{0}\right\rvert^{2}\neq 0for all[y0,y]∈ℂ​ℙn[y_{0},y]\in\mathbb{CP}^{n},
since not all homogeneous coordinatesy0,…,yny_{0},\ldots,y_{n}vanish simultaneously (as0∉ℂ​ℙn0\not\in\mathbb{CP}^{n}), and sinceAmA_{m}has ranknnasrank⁡(Al,∙)=n\operatorname{rank}(A_{l,\bullet})=nby definition ofAA. Consequently, the denominator never vanishes.

MoreoverF​(y,y¯)F(y,\overline{y})is a polynomial, hence smooth and|y0|2​s+2​k+2​ly0k​y0¯l​|y0|2​n+2=y0s−n−1+l​y0¯s−n−1+k,\frac{|y_{0}|^{2s+2k+2l}}{y_{0}^{k}\overline{y_{0}}^{l}|y_{0}|^{2n+2}}=y_{0}^{s-n-1+l}\ \overline{y_{0}}^{s-n-1+k},

which is also smooth, sinceRe⁡(s)>n+1\operatorname{Re}(s)>n+1. Hence, the entire integrand defines a smooth function onℂ​ℙn\mathbb{CP}^{n}.
Asℂ​ℙn\mathbb{CP}^{n}is compact and the integrand is smooth, it is bounded. Sinceℂ​ℙn\mathbb{CP}^{n}has finite volume,
the integral is finite. This proves the second part of the statement.
∎

By dominating the integrand ofeq.˜19with the functionggfromlemma˜6.5,
corresponding to the differential operatorP=PdlP=P_{d}^{l}, we may apply the Dominated Convergencetheorem˜B.2to interchange the limits and the integral.

## 7.A new formula for canonical forms - Theorem7.1

## Theorem 7.1.

Then:βL2​n−1=∑l∈[2​n]l≠d∑S,T∈ℐdl(−1)n+12+d+l+(l<d)Ψnsgn(S,T)det(AS​d,∙)det(AT​d,∙)perm(adj(M)S,T)Ω​(x)xd\displaystyle\beta^{2n-1}_{L}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in[2n]\\
l\neq d\end{subarray}}\sum_{S,T\in\mathcal{I}_{d}^{l}}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}+d+l+(l<d)}}{\Psi^{n}}\operatorname{sgn}(S,T)\det(A_{Sd,\bullet})\det(A_{Td,\bullet})\operatorname{perm}(\operatorname{adj}(M)_{S,T})\frac{\Omega(x)}{x_{d}}

In the second equality we used the Dodgson identityΨi1,i3​Ψi2,i4−Ψi1,i4​Ψi2,i3=Ψ​Ψ(i1,i2),(i3,i4)\Psi^{i_{1},i_{3}}\Psi^{i_{2},i_{4}}-\Psi^{i_{1},i_{4}}\Psi^{i_{2},i_{3}}=\Psi\Psi^{(i_{1},i_{2}),(i_{3},i_{4})}[25, Eq. 1.33]and in the last equality we evaluatedΨ(3,5),(2,4)=−x1\Psi^{(3,5),(2,4)}=-x_{1}.

To prove the theorem, we first express the two determinants in terms ofMM. This is established by the following lemma.

## Lemma 7.3.

LetS,T∈ℐdlS,T\in\mathcal{I}_{d}^{l}andΨ=det(M)\Psi=\det(M). Then,det(AS​d,∙)​det(AT​d,∙)=(−1)n+1​det(adj(M)S​l,T​l)Ψn−1.\det(A_{Sd,\bullet})\det(A_{Td,\bullet})=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{\det(\operatorname{adj}(M)_{Sl,Tl})}{\Psi^{n-1}}.

## Definition 7.5.

LetG​(S,T,l,σ,ϱ)=(VG,EG)G(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho)=(V_{G},E_{G})be the directed graph with verticesVG:=S⊔T⊔{l}V_{G}:=S\sqcup T\sqcup\{l\}and edgesEG:={(t~i,σ(s~i)∣i∈[n]}∪{(si,tϱ​(i))∣i∈[n−1]}E_{G}:=\{(\widetilde{t}_{i},\sigma(\widetilde{s}_{i})\mid i\in[n]\}\cup\{(s_{i},t_{\varrho(i)})\mid i\in[n-1]\}.
Then,∏(u,v)∈EGbu​v=∏i∈[n]bt~i​s~σ​(i)​∏j∈[n−1]bsj​tϱ​(j).\prod_{(u,v)\in E_{G}}b_{uv}=\prod_{i\in[n]}b_{\widetilde{t}_{i}\widetilde{s}_{\sigma(i)}}\prod_{j\in[n-1]}b_{s_{j}t_{\varrho(j)}}.

By construction, each vertex ofGGhas one incoming and one outgoing edge and is thus a disjoint union of directed cycles.
Moreover, the induced subgraph onS⊔TS\sqcup Tis bipartite, with edges oriented alternatively fromS→TS\to Tand fromT→ST\to S;
hence any cycle contained fully inS⊔TS\sqcup Thas even length.
The vertexllhas exactly one incoming edge fromTTand one outgoing edge toSS.
If one removeslland replaces these two edges by a single edge, the resulting cycle lies in the bipartite subgraphS⊔TS\sqcup Tand therefore
has even length. It follows that the original cycle containingllhas odd length.
We conclude:

## Fact 7.6.

The graphGGis a disjoint union of cycles. It contains exactly one odd cycle, namely the one containingll.
All other cycles (if any) are even and lie in the bipartite subgraphS⊔TS\sqcup T.

To illustrate this consider the following example:

## Example 7.7.

LetS={s1,s2}S=\{s_{1},s_{2}\},T={t1,t2}T=\{t_{1},t_{2}\}and letllbe the additional vertex.
Takeσ=(23)\sigma=(23)andϱ=id\varrho=\mathrm{id}. The corresponding graphGGis shown on the left infigure˜4.
If insteadσ~=(23)\widetilde{\sigma}=(23)andϱ=(12)\varrho=(12), the resulting graph is shown on the right.lls2s_{2}t2t_{2}s1s_{1}t1t_{1}lls2s_{2}t2t_{2}s1s_{1}t1t_{1}Figure 4.Graphs with distinct cycle structure arising from differentσ\sigmaandϱ\varrho.

In the first case the graph decomposes into two cycles: an odd cycle containingll, and an even cycle entirely contained inS⊔TS\sqcup T.
In contrast, the graph on the right consists of a single cycle of odd length.

The cancellations ineq.˜27are characterised as follows:

## Proposition 7.8.

The sum of all termsWd​(S,T,l,σ,ϱ)W_{d}(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho)ineq.˜27for whichG​(S,T,l,σ,ϱ)G(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho)contains an even cycle
vanishes.

## Proof.

To prove the result we define an involutionffon the space spanned by(S,T,l,σ,ϱ)(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho)such thatG​(S,T,l,σ,ϱ)G(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho)contains an even cycle, and show thatWd​(f​(S,T,l,σ,ϱ))=−Wd​(S,T,l,σ,ϱ)W_{d}(f(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho))=-W_{d}(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho).
It follows that in the sum over all such tuples(S,T,l,σ,ϱ)(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho)containing even cycles, the terms cancel pairwise and the sum vanishes.

Letc=(c1,…,c2​r)c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{2r})be the even cycle ofGGwhose smallest vertex is minimal amongst all even cycles and
orientccsuch thatc1c_{1}is the smallest vertex andc2<c2​rc_{2}<c_{2r}.

Sinceccis an even cycle, its vertices alternate betweenSSandTT. Define new ordered setsS′S^{\prime}andT′T^{\prime}by flipping the vertices ofccbetweenSSandTTby advancing them along the cycle by one. Concretely, if incca vertexx∈c∩Sx\in c\cap Sis followed byy∈c∩Ty\in c\cap T,
then, inTT,yyis replaced byxxand analogous ifx∈c∩Tx\in c\cap Tis followed byy∈∩Sy\in\cap S, then, inSS,yyis replaced byxx.
This is illustrated infigure˜5.35124687llSS:TT:35124687llS′S^{\prime}:T′T^{\prime}:Figure 5.Passing from(S,T)(S,T)to(S′,T′)(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})by advancing the cycle(158732)(158732)by one.

fixingnn. Then define new sets and permutationsS^=α​(S′),T^=β​(T′)as well asϱ^:=β−1∘ϱ∘α−1,σ^=α−1∘σ∘β−1.\widehat{S}=\alpha(S^{\prime}),\quad\widehat{T}=\beta(T^{\prime})\mathrel{\quad\text{as well as}\quad}\widehat{\varrho}:=\beta^{-1}\circ\varrho\circ\alpha^{-1},\quad\widehat{\sigma}=\alpha^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ\beta^{-1}.

This defines a mapf​(S,T,l,σ,ϱ)=(S^,T^,l,σ^,ϱ^).f(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho)=(\widehat{S},\widehat{T},l,\widehat{\sigma},\widehat{\varrho}).

To see thatffis an involution observe, that when applyingffa second time the same cycleccis being chosen and its elements
are being moved forward another step, that is they are being flipped back to their original positions. The resulting sorting permutations are exactly
the inverses of the ones from the first application. Thus,ffis a self-inverse bijection.

What remains to show is thatWd​(S^,T^,l,σ^,ϱ^)=−Wd​(S,T,l,σ,ϱ).W_{d}(\widehat{S},\widehat{T},l,\widehat{\sigma},\widehat{\varrho})=-W_{d}(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho).

Indeed, the transformation does not change the directed graph itself, it only relabels the cycle decomposition. Hence the monomial∏(u,v)∈EGbu​v=∏i∈[n]bt~i​s~σ​(i)​∏j∈[n−1]bsj​tϱ​(j).\prod_{(u,v)\in E_{G}}b_{uv}=\prod_{i\in[n]}b_{\widetilde{t}_{i}\widetilde{s}_{\sigma(i)}}\prod_{j\in[n-1]}b_{s_{j}t_{\varrho(j)}}.

is unchanged. It remains to compare signs. Observe thatsgn⁡(S^,T^)​sgn⁡(σ^)=sgn⁡(S′,T′)​sgn⁡(σ).\operatorname{sgn}(\widehat{S},\widehat{T})\operatorname{sgn}(\widehat{\sigma})=\operatorname{sgn}(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma).

as the only differences come from the sorting permutationsα\alphaandβ\betawhich appear twice and thus cancel.
It remains to consider the sign change from replacing(S,T)(S,T)by(S′,T′)(S^{\prime},T^{\prime}). If we concatenate the ordered setsSSandTTthen passing toS′S^{\prime}andT′T^{\prime}amounts to applying the cycle(c1​c2​…​c2​r)(c_{1}c_{2}\ldots c_{2r}). As this is an even cycle its sign is−1-1.
Thereforesgn⁡(S,T)=−sgn⁡(S′,T′)and henceWd​(S^,T^,l,σ^,ϱ^)=−Wd​(S,T,l,σ,ϱ),\operatorname{sgn}(S,T)=-\operatorname{sgn}(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\mathrel{\quad\text{and hence}\quad}W_{d}(\widehat{S},\widehat{T},l,\widehat{\sigma},\widehat{\varrho})=-W_{d}(S,T,l,\sigma,\varrho),

## Appendix ADodgson polynomials

Letn,m∈ℕn,m\in\mathbb{N}. In this section, we introduce several matrices and polynomials associated to graphs,
and more generally to the2​n×n2n\times n-matrices considered throughout this work, or even arbitrary realn×mn\times mmatrices.

Originally, these objects originate in graph theory and extend naturally to arbitrary matrices of the above form.
For this reason, we retain the terminology from the graph-theoretic setting, even though, for a general matrixAA,
the objects need not have any direct graph interpretation.

The correspondence between graphsGGand matricesAAis given by takingAAto be a reduced incidence matrix ofGG.
Roughly speaking, the rows ofAAcorrespond to edges of the graph, while the columns correspond to vertices.

## Definition A.1.

LetAAbe an×mn\times mmatrix, and letx=(x1,…,x2​n)x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2n})be homogeneous coordinates on the positive
coordinate simplexσn∈ℝ​ℙn−1\sigma_{n}\in\mathbb{RP}^{n-1}.

The Laplacian matrixLA​(x)L_{A}(x)and the graph matrixMA​(x)M_{A}(x)associated toAAare defined byLA​(x)=AT​diag⁡(x)​AMA​(x)=([c|c]​diag⁡(x)−AAT0).L_{A}(x)=A^{T}\operatorname{diag}(x)A\qquad M_{A}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}[c|c]\operatorname{diag}(x)&-A\\
\hline\cr A^{T}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Furthermore, we define the polynomialΨA​(x)=det(MA​(x))=det(LA​(x−1))​∏e∈[n]xe.\Psi_{A}(x)=\det(M_{A}(x))=\det(L_{A}(x^{-1}))\prod_{e\in[n]}x_{e}.

In the graph-theoretic settingΨA​(x)\Psi_{A}(x)is the first Symanzik polynomial.

## Remark A.2.

For brevity, we will often omit the dependence onAAandxxfrom the notation and simply writeLL,MM, andΨ\Psiinstead ofLA​(x)L_{A}(x),MA​(x)M_{A}(x), andΨA​(x)\Psi_{A}(x).
The parametersAAandxxwill only be included when necessary for clarity or emphasis.

As a generalisation ofΨ\Psi, one may also consider minors of the graph matrixMM.

## Definition A.3.

LetI,J⊆[n+m]I,J\subseteq[n+m]with|I|=|J|\left\lvert I\right\rvert=\left\lvert J\right\rvert. The determinant of the submatrix ofMMobtained by removing the rows indexed byIIand the columns indexed byJJ,ΨI,J=det(MI,J)\Psi^{I,J}=\det(M^{I,J})

is called a Dodgson polynomial ofMM.
In the special case whereI={i}I=\{i\}andJ={j}J=\{j\}consist of single elements, we writeΨi,j\Psi^{i,j}instead ofΨ{i},{j}\Psi^{\{i\},\{j\}}.

Recall that the matrixMMnaturally decomposes into two types of rows and columns: the firstnncorrespond to rows ofAA,
while the remainingmmcorrespond to the columns ofAA. Accordingly we call an indexi∈[n+m]i\in[n+m], a row index ifi≤ni\leq n, and
a column index ifi>ni>n.
Row indices will be denoted byee, and column indices byvv, following the graph-theoretic interpretation
in which they correspond to edges and vertices, respectively.

The following symmetry properties follow immediately from the block structure ofMM:

## Fact A.4.

IfIIandJJconsist entirely of row indices or entirely of column indices, thenΨI,J=ΨJ,I\Psi^{I,J}=\Psi^{J,I}.
However ifeeis a row index andvvis a column index, thenΨe,v=−Ψv,e\Psi^{e,v}=-\Psi^{v,e}.

Next, we discuss several key identities relating Dodgson polynomials, the LaplacianLL, and the graph matrixMM.
All of these identities are already known in the literature, and we indicate where they were originally proved.
However, since many of them were established only in the graph-theoretic setting,
we also provide proofs in the more general context of arbitraryn×mn\times mmatricesAA.

First we can express the entries ofLA−1​(x−1)L_{A}^{-1}(x^{-1})in terms of Dodgson polynomials:

## Lemma A.5([15, Lemma 3.9]).

Let(u,v)∈[m]2(u,v)\in[m]^{2}, then(LA−1​(x−1))u,v=(−1)u+v​Ψn+u,n+vΨ.(L_{A}^{-1}(x^{-1}))_{u,v}=(-1)^{u+v}\frac{\Psi^{n+u,n+v}}{\Psi}.

## Proof.

Denote thek×kk\times kidentity matrix byIkI_{k}and define the diagonal matrixD:=diag⁡(x1,…,xn)D:=\operatorname{diag}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}).
The minorMn+u,n+vM^{n+u,n+v}can be decomposed asM=F​G​HM=FGH, whereF=([c|c]​In0(AT)u,∙​D−1Im−1)G=([c|c]​D00(LA​(x−1))u,v)H=([c|c]​In−D−1​A∙,v0Im−1).F=\begin{pmatrix}[c|c]I_{n}&0\\
\hline\cr(A^{T})^{u,\bullet}D^{-1}&I_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}\qquad G=\begin{pmatrix}[c|c]D&0\\
\hline\cr 0&(L_{A}(x^{-1}))^{u,v}\end{pmatrix}\qquad H=\begin{pmatrix}[c|c]I_{n}&-D^{-1}A^{\bullet,v}\\
\hline\cr 0&I_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Thus we find thatΨn+u,n+vΨ=det(Mn+u,n+v)Ψ=det(F​G​H)Ψ=det((LA​(x−1))u,v)​∏e∈[n]xeΨ=(LA−1​(x−1))u,v\frac{\Psi^{n+u,n+v}}{\Psi}=\frac{\det(M^{n+u,n+v})}{\Psi}=\frac{\det(FGH)}{\Psi}=\det((L_{A}(x^{-1}))^{u,v})\frac{\prod_{e\in[n]}x_{e}}{\Psi}=(L_{A}^{-1}(x^{-1}))_{u,v}

where in the last equality we used thatdet(LA​(x−1))=Ψ/∏e∈[n]xe\det(L_{A}(x^{-1}))=\Psi/\prod_{e\in[n]}x_{e}as well asLi​j−1=det(Li​j)det(L)L_{ij}^{-1}=\frac{\det(L^{ij})}{\det(L)}for an invertible matrixLL.
∎

Next, we may reformulate an identity due to Jacobi to
writeΨI,J\Psi^{I,J}as sums over products of Dodgson polynomialsΨu,v\Psi^{u,v}.

## Lemma A.6([11, Lemma 48]).

LetI,J⊆[n]I,J\subseteq[n]be sets of row indices of equal cardinality. ThenΨI,J=det(MI,J)=σ​(I)​σ​(J)det(M)|I|−1det(adj(M)I,J)\Psi^{I,J}=\det(M^{I,J})=\frac{\sigma(I)\sigma(J)}{\det(M)^{\left\lvert I\right\rvert-1}}\det(\operatorname{adj}(M)_{I,J})

whereσ​(I)=(−1)i1+…+ik​sgn⁡(I)\sigma(I)=(-1)^{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{k}}\operatorname{sgn}(I).

A proof of this identity can be found in[8, Lemma 28].
There is also an explicit expression for the Dodgson polynomialsΨI,J\Psi^{I,J}in terms of the matrixAA:

## Lemma A.7([8, Proof of Proposition 23]).

LetI,J⊆[n]I,J\subseteq[n]of equal cardinality and
letQQbe the set of subsets of[n]∖{I∪J}[n]\setminus\{I\cup J\}of cardinalityn−m−|I|n-m-\left\lvert I\right\rvert,
ThenΨI,J=∑R∈Q(−1)∑e∈R|I<e|+|J<e|​det(AR+I,∙)​det(AR+J,∙)​∏e∈Rxe.\Psi^{I,J}=\sum_{R\in Q}(-1)^{\sum_{e\in R}\left\lvert I_{<e}\right\rvert+\left\lvert J_{<e}\right\rvert}\det(A^{R+I,\bullet})\det(A^{R+J,\bullet})\prod_{e\in R}x_{e}.

In the caseR=∅R=\varnothing, the product∏e∈Rxe\prod_{e\in R}x_{e}is understood to equal11.

## Proof.

Since the lower-rightm×mm\times msubmatrix ofMMis zero, any nonzero term indet(MI,J)\det(M^{I,J})must
choosemmrows/columns from each submatrixAA.
After removing the rows indexed byIIand the columns indexed byJJ, this leavesn−m−|I|n-m-\left\lvert I\right\rvertdiagonal entries from the submatrixDDto be chosen.

LetQQdenote the set of all subsetsR⊆[n]∖(I∪J)R\subseteq[n]\setminus(I\cup J)of cardinalityn−m−|I|n-m-\left\lvert I\right\rvert.
Expanding along the diagonal submatrixDDgivesΨI,J=det(MI,J)=∑R∈Q(−1)∑e∈R|I<e|+|J<e|​|0−AJ+R,∙(AI+R,∙)T0|​∏e∈Rxe.\Psi^{I,J}=\det(M^{I,J})=\sum_{R\in Q}(-1)^{\sum_{e\in R}\left\lvert I_{<e}\right\rvert+\left\lvert J_{<e}\right\rvert}\begin{vmatrix}0&-A^{J+R,\bullet}\\
(A^{I+R,\bullet})^{T}&0\end{vmatrix}\prod_{e\in R}x_{e}.

where the sign is the usual Laplace sign arising from selecting the diagonal entries,
giving(−1)e−|I<e|+e−|J<e|(-1)^{e-\left\lvert I_{<e}\right\rvert+e-\left\lvert J_{<e}\right\rvert}for eachxex_{e}.
Finally, to bring the remaining matrix into block diagonal form to write it as the product of determinants, one permutes rows and columns.
The sign of this permutation is cancelled by the minus sign in the upper-right block, and the formula follows.
∎

## Remark A.8.

In the case ofSShaving cardinalityn−mn-m,RRis just an empty set and the formula reduces toΨI,J=det(AI,∙)​det(AJ,∙),\Psi^{I,J}=\det(A^{I,\bullet})\det(A^{J,\bullet}),

so that the Dodgson polynomialΨI,J\Psi^{I,J}is actually just±1\pm 1.

Moreover, for the case of graphs,
only the terms whereR+SR+SandR+TR+Tare spanning tree compliments contribute as else one of the determinants ofAAwill vanish.

Finally, we have an identity relating column indexed to row indexed Dodgson polynomials. For this we first need the following additional lemma:

## Lemma A.9([3, Lemma A.11]).

Lete∈[n]e\in[n]be a row index and letv∈[n+m]v\in[n+m], be a row or column index distinct fromee. Then∑j∈[m](−1)j​ae​j​ψj,v=(−1)e+n−1​xe​Ψe,v.\sum_{j\in[m]}(-1)^{j}a_{ej}\psi^{j,v}=(-1)^{e+n-1}x_{e}\Psi^{e,v}.

## Proof.

The proof of this theorem is basically an exercise in Laplace expansion after one observes that the structure of theee-th column is given
by one entryxex_{e}in theee-th row and then the entriesae​ja_{ej}in the rowsn+1n+1ton+mn+m.

First consider the minor ofMe,vM^{e,v}corresponding toΨe,v\Psi^{e,v}and expand it along theee-th column to obtain:Ψe,v=∑j=1m(−1)e+(n−1+j)​ae​j​Ψ{e,j},{e,v}thus(−1)e+n−1​xe​Ψe,v=∑j=1m(−1)j​ae​j​xe​Ψ{e,j},{e,v}.\Psi^{e,v}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(-1)^{e+(n-1+j)}a_{ej}\Psi^{\{e,j\},\{e,v\}}\mathrel{\quad\text{thus}\quad}(-1)^{e+n-1}x_{e}\Psi^{e,v}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(-1)^{j}a_{ej}x_{e}\Psi^{\{e,j\},\{e,v\}}.

Letj∈[m]j\in[m], then we can consider the minorMj,vM^{j,v}corresponding toΨj,v\Psi^{j,v}and expand it along theee-th column to obtain:Ψj,v=(−1)e+e​xe​Ψ{e,j},{e,v}+∑k∈[m]∖{j}(−1)e+n+k−(j<k)​ae​k​Ψ{j,k},{e,v},\Psi^{j,v}=(-1)^{e+e}x_{e}\Psi^{\{e,j\},\{e,v\}}+\sum_{k\in[m]\setminus\{j\}}(-1)^{e+n+k-(j<k)}a_{ek}\Psi^{\{j,k\},\{e,v\}},

where the(j<k)(j<k)in the sign arises from the fact that ifjjis smaller thankkthen in the submatrixMj,vM^{j,v}thekk-th row ofMMappears in positionk−1k-1, whereas ifj>kj>kthen thekk-th row is still thekk-th row.
Let us then consider∑j∈[m](−1)j​ae​j​Ψj,v=∑j∈[m](−1)j​ae​j​xe​Ψ{e,j},{e,v}​∑j,k=1j≠km(−1)e+n+k+j−(j<k)​ae​j​ae​k​Ψ{j,k},{e,v}.\sum_{j\in[m]}(-1)^{j}a_{ej}\Psi^{j,v}=\sum_{j\in[m]}(-1)^{j}a_{ej}x_{e}\Psi^{\{e,j\},\{e,v\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k=1\\
j\neq k\end{subarray}}^{m}(-1)^{e+n+k+j-(j<k)}a_{ej}a_{ek}\Psi^{\{j,k\},\{e,v\}}.

Here the second sum vanishes as the term(j,k)(j,k)and(k,j)(k,j)appear with opposite sign.
Comparing the remaining term in the expansion of the sum overΨj,v\Psi^{j,v}with the expansion ofΨe,v\Psi^{e,v}proves the lemma.
∎

Using the previous lemma, the identity between row indexed and column indexed Dodgson polynomials is given by:

## Lemma A.10([3, Lemma A.12]).

Lete1,e2∈[n]e_{1},e_{2}\in[n]be distinct row indices. Then∑j,k∈[m](−1)j+k​ae1​j​ae2​k​Ψj,k=(−1)e1+e2+1​xe1​xe2​Ψe1,e2.\sum_{j,k\in[m]}(-1)^{j+k}a_{e_{1}j}a_{e_{2}k}\Psi^{j,k}=(-1)^{e_{1}+e_{2}+1}x_{e_{1}}x_{e_{2}}\Psi^{e_{1},e_{2}}.

which can be summarised as(A​L​(x)−1​AT)e1​e2=−xe1​xe2​M​(x)e1​e2−1,(AL(x)^{-1}A^{T})_{e_{1}e_{2}}=-x_{e_{1}}x_{e_{2}}M(x)^{-1}_{e_{1}e_{2}},

## Proof.

Consider the right hand side and applylemma˜A.9once withe=e1e=e_{1}andv=e2v=e_{2}to obtain:(−1)e1+e2+1​xe1​xe2​Ψe1​e2=∑j∈[m]ae1​j​(−1)j+e2+n​xe2​Ψj,e2.(-1)^{e_{1}+e_{2}+1}x_{e_{1}}x_{e_{2}}\Psi^{e_{1}e_{2}}=\sum_{j\in[m]}a_{e_{1}j}(-1)^{j+e_{2}+n}x_{e_{2}}\Psi^{j,e_{2}}.

Now take the(−1)e2+n−1​xe2​Ψj,e2(-1)^{e_{2}+n-1}x_{e_{2}}\Psi^{j,e_{2}}in every summand and applylemma˜A.9withe=e2e=e_{2}andv=jv=j.
Notice that asΨe,v=−Ψv,e\Psi^{e,v}=-\Psi^{v,e}we obtain a negative sign,∑j∈[m]ae1​j​(−1)j+e2+n​xe2​Ψj,e2=∑j∈[m]∑k∈[m](−1)j+k​ae1​j​ae2​k​Ψk,j.\sum_{j\in[m]}a_{e_{1}j}(-1)^{j+e_{2}+n}x_{e_{2}}\Psi^{j,e_{2}}=\sum_{j\in[m]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in[m]\end{subarray}}(-1)^{j+k}a_{e_{1}j}a_{e_{2}k}\Psi^{k,j}.

As bothjjandkkare column indicesΨj,k=Ψk,j\Psi^{j,k}=\Psi^{k,j}, which proves the first identity.

Letv∈[m]v\in[m], then(L​(x−1)−1​AT)v​e2=∑k=1mae2​k​(L−1​(x−1))v​k(L(x^{-1})^{-1}A^{T})_{ve_{2}}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{e_{2}k}(L^{-1}(x^{-1}))_{vk}

and for the full expansion:(A​L​(x−1)−1​AT)e1​e2=∑j,k∈[m]ae1​j​ae2​k​(L−1​(x−1))j​k=∑j,k∈[m](−1)j+k​ae1​j​ae2​k​Ψj,kΨ,(AL(x^{-1})^{-1}A^{T})_{e_{1}e_{2}}=\sum_{j,k\in[m]}a_{e_{1}j}a_{e_{2}k}(L^{-1}(x^{-1}))_{jk}=\sum_{j,k\in[m]}(-1)^{j+k}a_{e_{1}j}a_{e_{2}k}\frac{\Psi^{j,k}}{\Psi},

where in the second equality we usedlemma˜A.5.
Applying the first identity we obtain:∑j,k∈[m](−1)j+k​ae1​j​ae2​k​Ψj,kΨ=(−1)e1+e2+1​xe1​xe2​Ψe1,e2Ψ=−xe1​xe2​(M​(x)−1)e1​e2.\sum_{j,k\in[m]}(-1)^{j+k}a_{e_{1}j}a_{e_{2}k}\frac{\Psi^{j,k}}{\Psi}=(-1)^{e_{1}+e_{2}+1}x_{e_{1}}x_{e_{2}}\frac{\Psi^{e_{1},e_{2}}}{\Psi}=-x_{e_{1}}x_{e_{2}}(M(x)^{{}^{-1}})_{e_{1}e_{2}}.

which proves the second identity.
∎

## Appendix BFubinis theorem, Leibniz integral rule and dominated convergence

We summarise three classic results from measure theory which we frequently use in the main text.
They allow us to exchange limits and derivatives with integrals as well as exchange integrals themselves.

## Theorem B.1(Fubini’s Theorem).

LetX,YX,Ybeσ\sigma-finite measure spaces and suppose thatX×YX\times Yhas the product measure.
Ifffis a measureable function and its integral overX×YX\times Yconverges absolutely, that is∫X×Y|f​(x,y)|​d​(x,y)<∞\int_{X\times Y}\left\lvert f(x,y)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,y)<\infty

then∫X∫Yf​(x,y)​d​y​d​y=∫Y∫Xf​(x,y)​d​x​d​y=∫X×Y|f​(x,y)|​d​(x,y).\int_{X}\int_{Y}f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{Y}\int_{X}f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{X\times Y}\left\lvert f(x,y)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(x,y).

## Theorem B.2(Dominated Convergence Theorem (DCT)).

LetXXbe an open subset ofℝ\mathbb{R},Ω\Omegaa measure space and fixx0∈Xx_{0}\in X.
Letf:X×Ω→ℂf:X\times\Omega\to\mathbb{C}be a complex-valued measureable function such that:
- (1)

For almost allω∈Ω\omega\in\Omegalimx→x0f​(x,ω)=f​(x0,ω).\lim_{x\to x_{0}}f(x,\omega)=f(x_{0},\omega).
- (2)

There exists a integrable functiong:Ω→ℝg:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}such that for allx∈Xx\in Xand almost allω∈Ω\omega\in\Omega|f​(x,ω)|<g​(ω)\left\lvert f(x,\omega)\right\rvert<g(\omega).

thenlimx→x0∫Ωf​(x,ω)​d​ω=∫Ωf​(x0,ω)​d​ω.\lim_{x\to x_{0}}\int_{\Omega}f(x,\omega)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega=\int_{\Omega}f(x_{0},\omega)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega.

## Theorem B.3(Leibniz integral rule).

LetXXbe an open subset ofℝ\mathbb{R},Ω\Omegaa measure space andf:X×Ω→ℂf:X\times\Omega\to\mathbb{C}a function such that:
- (1)

For eachx∈Xx\in Xf​(x,ω)f(x,\omega)is an integrable function ofω\omega.
- (2)

For almost allω∈Ω\omega\in\Omega, the partial derivative∂f∂x\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}exists for allx∈Xx\in X.
- (3)

There exists an integrable functiong:Ω→ℝg:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}such that for allx∈Xx\in Xand almost allω∈Ω\omega\in\Omega|∂f∂x​(x,ω)|<g​(ω)\left\lvert\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(x,\omega)\right\rvert<g(\omega).

Then forx∈Xx\in X∂∂x​∫Ωf​(x,ω)​d​ω=∫Ω∂f∂x​(x,ω)​d​ω.\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\int_{\Omega}f(x,\omega)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(x,\omega)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega.

## Appendix CDivided differences

We also briefly use a key theorem from the theory of divided differences.
As a reference for this we use[27].

## Definition C.1.

Letn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}andI⊆ℝI\subseteq\mathbb{R}and interval.
Letf:I→ℝf:I\to\mathbb{R}benn-times differentiable andx0,…,xnx_{0},\ldots,x_{n}ben+1n+1points inIIthen thedivided differenceofffis defined asf​[xk]\displaystyle f[x_{k}]:=f​(xk),\displaystyle:=f(x_{k}),k∈{0,…,n}\displaystyle k\in\{0,\ldots,n\}f​[xk,…,xj]\displaystyle f[x_{k},\ldots,x_{j}]:=f​[xk+1,…,xj]−f​[xk,…,xj−1]xj−xk,\displaystyle:={\frac{f[x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{j}]-f[x_{k},\ldots,x_{j-1}]}{x_{j}-x_{k}}},k∈{0,…,n−1},j∈{k+1,…,n}.\displaystyle k\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\},\ j\in\{k+1,\ldots,n\}.

By definingf​[x0,…,x0]=f(n)​(x0)n!f[x_{0},\ldots,x_{0}]=\frac{f^{(n)}(x_{0})}{n!}

this definition can be continuously extended to the points where somexjx_{j}coincide.

An explicit non-recursive formula for the divided difference (whenever no points coincide) is given by

## Proposition C.2.

Letffas above and letx0,…,xnx_{0},\ldots,x_{n}be distinct. Thenf​[x0,…,xn]=∑j=0nf​(xj)∏k=0k≠jn(xj−xk)f[x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}]=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\frac{f(x_{j})}{\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=0\\
k\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}(x_{j}-x_{k})}

The key theorem we use in this work is the following:

## Theorem C.3(Mean value theorem for divided differences).

There exists a pointξ\xiin the interior ofIIsuch thatf​[x0,…,xn]=fn​(ξ)n!.f[x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}]=\frac{f^{n}(\xi)}{n!}.
